## Extension — 2026-11-08 03:31 — E2b done: the engine by ranges and splices; walkthrough

**Summary**: The plan of 03:10, as approved at 03:16, in its four steps. The tag engine is given a
file's first bytes and its last 160, never its audio, and a save returns a splice (the file's
first `tags_end` bytes replaced with new tags). The module's memory after reading and saving a
150 MB FLAC file went from 151 / 301 MiB (E3) to 1.2 MiB. Every fixture gives the same bytes by
the head-and-splice path as by the whole-file one; E2's and E3's checks in Chromium pass again.

### What was done

1. **`jackbird-tags`**:
   - `layout(head, tail, len) -> Result<Layout, Error>`: `Layout::Known(FileLayout)` or
     `Layout::NeedHead(n)`. `FileLayout { format, tags_end, audio }`. Constants `FIRST_HEAD`
     (64 KiB) and `TAIL_LEN` (160).
   - `read_head(head, &FileLayout)` and `write_head(head, &FileLayout, &Edit) -> Splice`
     (`Splice { replace: 0..tags_end, with }`).
   - The three safeguards on the tags: (1) and (3) as before; (2) is now structural, the new tags
     must end exactly where their own header (MP3) or last block (FLAC) says
     (`region::declared_end`), so the source's bytes after `tags_end` follow them unchanged.
   - Whole-file `read`, `write` and `audio_region` stay, built on the same pieces; `write` also
     checks the whole result's audio region byte for byte. The region's rule lives in one private
     function, `region()`, which both `audio_region` and `layout` use.
   - Three departures from the plan's sketch, none in substance:
     - The head functions are `read_head` and `write_head`, and the whole-file ones keep their
       names (`read`, `write`), so no caller or test of E1 had to change.
     - `FileLayout.format` is an `Option`: a file the engine does not read still has its region
       (the MP3 rule, as `audio_region` always gave it), so the checksums of any file in the walk
       are found as before.
     - `verify` (whole files) is now test-only, composed of the same three checks a save runs.
2. **`jackbird-tags-wasm`**: exports `tags_layout`, `tags_read` and `tags_write` over the new
   requests (`wire.rs` describes them); `tags_region` is gone. Lengths travel as `u32`
   (a larger file is refused with `too-large`; the browser cannot hold one anyway).
3. **The web app**:
   - `src/lib/tags/engine.ts` drives an `Engine` on a whole file in memory: `layoutOf` (the tail
     and a head that starts at 64 KiB and grows to what the engine asks, at least doubling each
     time, so a FLAC file with many large pictures needs few steps), `readOf` and `writeOf`
     (only the first `tagsEnd` bytes go to the module; the new file is the splice's bytes, then
     the source's own from `tagsEnd`; the same bytes when nothing changes; a splice that does not
     replace exactly the tags given is refused).
   - `src/lib/tags/index.ts` keeps one layout per buffer (`WeakMap`), so the scan's checksums and
     tags of one file find it once. `writeTags` checks end to end, before giving the file back,
     that the new file's audio region hashes as the source's did (`refused-audio` otherwise).
   - `src/helpers/tagwire.ts` mirrors the new messages.
4. **Rules and docs**: `AGENTS.md` ("They do no I/O: they are given the ranges of a file they ask
   for (its head and its last bytes) and return what to write (a splice …); the audio never
   passes through them"), `docs/architecture.md`, `STACK.md`; the roadmap gains E2b, done.

### Checks

- `cargo fmt --check`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` and the same on
  `jackbird-tags-wasm` for `wasm32-unknown-unknown`: clean. `cargo test --workspace --locked`:
  84 pass. New ones:
  - `layout` on every fixture: its format, `tags_end` (from `jackbird-id3`'s or
    `jackbird-flac`'s own reading) and region (from `cases.json`), in one step.
  - From **every** head length of every fixture (0 to its size, byte by byte), following what
    `layout` asks for always ends at the same layout as from the whole file, and every request is
    larger than what was given and no larger than the file.
  - The head-and-splice path, given only the first `tags_end` bytes, gives the same result as
    whole-file `write` on every fixture for five edits (a title; every field and a cover; the
    cover removed; two fields emptied; nothing): the same bytes, or the same error.
  - The structural safeguard refusing new tags with a byte too many after them, and tags a byte
    short; `layout` asking for 10 bytes, then the tag and 4, then knowing; a head shorter than
    the tags, or a tail that is not the file's, refused.
  - The module's exports through their buffers; the wire format both ways.
- `npm test`: 308 pass, the coverage check passes with 19 helpers at 90% or more. New: the
  module is never given more than 64 KiB + 160 bytes of a 32 MB file to read and save it; a head
  that has to grow past a 300 KB cover; a lying splice refused.
- **Memory**, the same module in Node, fresh for each file (`.tmp/scripts/e2b-memory.mts`):

  | File | Tags | After reading | After saving a title | After saving a 1.3 MB cover |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | FLAC, 150 MB, no picture | 8 KB | 1.2 MiB (E3: 151) | 1.2 MiB (E3: 301) | 9.1 MiB |
  | MP3, 13.35 MB, 1.3 MB cover | 1.3 MB | 5.1 MiB | 11.7 MiB | 11.7 MiB |

  Reading the 150 MB file's tags takes 1.2 ms (E3: 45 ms); a save, 120 ms (E3: 389 ms), most
  of it JavaScript copying the 150 MB into the new file.
- **Chromium**, the built app (E3's and E2's scripts, run again): it opens a library of FLAC and
  MP3 files, plays the 24/96 FLAC, Info says FLAC; the four FLAC saves and the three MP3 saves
  pass as before (the invalid v2.3 year still refused, by name); each saved file has its source's
  audio MD5 under `ffmpeg -map 0:a`, its audio bytes, its other blocks, its unedited comments and
  its vendor string; a reopen from `.meta/` requests no `.wasm`. The module is 105.3 KB raw.

### What stays as it was

The scan and the verifier still read each new or changed file whole, in JavaScript, because the
full checksum hashes all of it; that copy is collected after. A save still builds the new file in
JavaScript and writes it whole through the VFS (the owner's choice at 03:16). The module only
ever gets the tags.

### Next

By the roadmap: E4, the Tauri v2 desktop app, which can now read only a file's head and tail from
disk and write the splice in place. Awaiting the owner's go.

Suggested commit message: `feat(tags): the engine by ranges and splices, never given the audio; E2b`.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 10:52 — E4, the Tauri v2 desktop app: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for E4 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/rust-tag-engine-and-tauri.md`. Before it, the
owner raised a desktop app with two modes, a window and a background service (*"me gustaría tener
2 modos: UI y servicio"*); asked how it had been planned (one app with its core in process, E-D7:
no daemon; the server is the family instance, F0–F5, after E4), the owner chose to keep the plan
as it was (*"Sigamos con el plan como estaba"*). The service mode is noted below as the owner's
idea, deferred. E4 makes the desktop app: the same interface in Tauri's webview, files reached
natively by path, tags and checksums in Rust reading only what they need, playback streaming from
disk, and bundles for macOS, Windows and Linux. Eight decisions are proposed; three questions are
left to the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- `src-tauri/` is E0's shell (`tauri` 2.12.5, `tauri-plugin-dialog` 2.8.4, edition 2021, its own
  Cargo project), with the E0 check page (`src-tauri/e0/`) and `tauri-shell.yml`. E0 found
  (2026-11-04 18:55 to 19:41): jq79 draws the app in all three webviews; no File System Access API
  in WebKit (macOS, Linux); `<audio>` plays from the asset protocol on macOS and Windows (with
  `crossOrigin` for the analyser) but not on Linux, where a local HTTP server with byte ranges plays.
  The 19:41 proposal for media URLs is still awaiting approval (question 1).
- The web app reaches files through `@cloudauthn/vfs-sync`'s `VFSAdapter` (`FSAAdapter`); a root
  keeps its `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` (`rootHandle`) for playback (`src/lib/media.ts`) and the
  session (`rootSessionStore.ts`, IndexedDB; `Session<Handle>` is already generic).
- `src/lib/tags/` works on bytes (`readTags(bytes)`, `writeTags(bytes, …)`, `checksums(bytes)`),
  which the scan, the verifier and the editor read whole through the VFS. Over Tauri's IPC that
  would ship whole files to the webview and back, which E-D6 rules out.
- The engine works on ranges since E2b: `layout(head, tail, len)`, `read_head`, `write_head` →
  `Splice`.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| E4-D1 | **One seam for where a root lives**, `src/lib/sources/`: a `RootSource` gives a root's `vfs`, its media URL for a path, and what the session keeps of it (a handle, a path). `browser.ts` is today's (FSA, handles, object URLs); `desktop.ts` is Tauri's. `src/lib/platform.ts` says which runs (`isTauri`), once. `RootWorkspace.rootHandle` becomes `source`. The views do not change. | one place decides; the same `dist/` runs in the browser and in the app |
| E4-D2 | **Tags as file operations**: `src/lib/tags/` gains `inspect(root, path, { tags })` (size, date, both checksums, and the tags when asked) and `save(root, path, previous, metadata)` (the new size, date and checksums). The web implementation is today's (the VFS reads the bytes, the wasm does the work); the desktop one is one IPC call each, with no file bytes crossing. The scan, the verifier and the editor call these instead of reading bytes. | E-D6: no whole files over the IPC; the "operations, not bytes" seam this record asked for in August (§3, the storage seam) |
| E4-D3 | **Native commands** (`src-tauri/src/`): a registry of open roots (only folders the person chose in the dialog, or restored from the app's own session, can be reached); `walk` (the whole tree with sizes and dates in one call), `read`, `write`, `stat`, `rename`, `delete`, `mkdir` for small files (`.meta/`, playlists) and renames; `inspect` and `save` with `jackbird-tags` natively. Every path is relative to a root, resolved (links included) and refused if it leaves it. | the browser's permission model, by folder, kept on the desktop |
| E4-D4 | **Native tags and checksums read only what they need**: `inspect` reads the head and the tail for the tags (`layout`, `read_head`), and hashes the file in 1 MiB chunks, streaming, for both SHA-1s (the `sha1` crate of RustCrypto, a dependency of `src-tauri/` only, not of `crates/`). `save` gets the splice from `write_head`; when the new tags are the same length (the engine reuses padding, so most edits), it writes them in place and the audio is never touched; when they are not, see question 2. After a save, the audio region is hashed again and compared with the source's. | a 150 MB FLAC is retitled by writing a few KB; nothing is held in memory |
| E4-D5 | **Media URLs** as proposed at 19:41: the asset protocol on macOS and Windows, its scope limited at run time to the open roots (not E0's `**`); on Linux, a local HTTP server in the Rust side (127.0.0.1, a random port, a per-session token in every URL, only files under the open roots, byte ranges, CORS for the app's origin alone), started only on Linux. The `<audio>` element always gets `crossOrigin = "anonymous"`. | E0's results; question 1 |
| E4-D6 | **Session on paths**: on the desktop, `Session<string>` (each root's path) in the same IndexedDB store; restoring registers the paths with the native side and reopens them, no permission prompt (the app's own paths). A path that is gone is skipped, as a revoked handle is today. | "reopens it after a restart" |
| E4-D7 | **One workspace, pinned versions**: `src-tauri/` joins the root Cargo workspace (edition 2024, the workspace's lints, `unsafe_code` relaxed only if Tauri's macros need it, as `tags-wasm` does, and said so) and depends on `jackbird-tags` by path. The JavaScript side gets `@tauri-apps/api` and `@tauri-apps/plugin-dialog`, pinned to the Rust versions. E0's check page and `tauri-shell.yml` retire; their findings stay in this record. | one `cargo test`; the engine the app ships is the one the tests run |
| E4-D8 | **Builds**: `desktop.yml` builds the app on Linux (Ubuntu 22.04, `.deb` and `.AppImage`), macOS (`.dmg`) and Windows (NSIS) on pull requests that touch it, runs an unattended check on each (the app opens a fixture folder from a test switch, reads its tags, saves one, plays a track a few seconds, and reports; Linux under Xvfb), and on a release attaches the bundles to the GitHub release (`release.yml`). Unsigned (question 3). | the roadmap's "release builds for macOS, Windows and Linux in GitHub Actions" |

### Not in E4

- **The service mode** (the owner's idea, 2026-11-08): the desktop app running without a window, in
  the background, serving the library. Kept for later; when it comes, it is likely the family
  instance's server (F1) in its "one person, open" mode (21:35) sharing the app's core, which
  E4-D2 and E4-D3 keep possible by putting the work behind file operations in Rust.
- Automatic updates, watching folders for changes, native audio output (E5), mobile (E6), the
  server (F0–F5), the Musikmatch rename.

### Tests and checks

- `cargo test --workspace`: the native commands on a temporary folder: a path leaving its root
  (`..`, an absolute path, a link out) refused; `walk`; `inspect` against `cases.json` (checksums,
  region, tags) on every fixture; `save` on every writable fixture giving the same bytes as
  `jackbird_tags::write` on the whole file, in place and by the other route of question 2; the
  Linux media server's token, root check, ranges and CORS.
- `npm test`: the seams (`platform.ts` and the web `inspect` and `save` over a memory VFS) stay
  at the floors; the browser app unchanged (E2b's and E3's Chromium checks again).
- By hand here: the Linux build under Xvfb (WebKitGTK), as E0 did: open the FLAC and MP3 library
  of E3, play, edit and save, close, reopen.
- CI: the three systems, unattended (E4-D8). By the owner, on real machines: the bundles opened,
  a folder chosen, heard, edited, the app restarted.

### Steps (estimates)

1. The seams in TypeScript (`platform.ts`, `sources/`, tags as file operations), the browser
   behaviour unchanged (1.5 d).
2. `src-tauri/` into the workspace; the roots registry, `walk` and the small-file commands (1.5 d).
3. Native `inspect` and `save`, streaming hashes, the splice written (2 d).
4. The desktop source, its `VFSAdapter` over the commands, the dialog, the session on paths (1 d).
5. Media URLs: the asset protocol's scope, the Linux server (1.5 d).
6. `desktop.yml`, the unattended check, the release's bundles (1.5 d).
7. The checks by hand, the docs, the walkthrough, the roadmap (1 d).

### Done when

E4's row: the desktop app opens a folder, plays, edits and reopens it after a restart on all three
systems.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Media URLs** (E4-D5): the 19:41 proposal, the asset protocol on macOS and Windows and a local
   server on Linux (recommended: nothing listens where it is not needed), or the local server on
   all three (one path, but a port open on every system, and macOS's run never finished in E0)?
2. **A save whose tags change size**: write the new file beside the old one and rename it over
   (recommended: a crash or a full disk leaves the old file whole; it costs one copy of the file
   on disk, streamed, not in memory), or shift the audio in place, as lofty and rust-id3 do (no
   copy, but a crash midway leaves the file broken)?
3. **Signing**: unsigned bundles for now (recommended: macOS and Windows warn when they are
   opened; signing needs an Apple Developer ID and a Windows certificate, the owner's accounts and
   costs), or set signing up in E4?

## Extension — 2026-11-08 13:55 — The background service as the owner describes it; what it changes in E4 (proposal)

**Summary**: The owner set out the service's shape (2026-11-08). It is a binary of its own; the
web and the desktop app are its two interface clients; the desktop app carries the core itself
(its own backend) and offers, from its interface, to install the service; how the service starts
depends on the system. This replaces the guess of 10:52 ("not in E4") about what the service
would be. What it asks of E4 now is small: the native core as a crate with no Tauri in it, so the
service can be built on it later. Awaiting approval, with E4's plan; open questions below.

User message (translated): *"The background service would be an independent binary. It would have
two interface clients: web and desktop. The desktop app would include the core (its own backend).
From the desktop interface there would be an option to install the service. How the service
starts depends on the system."*

### The shape

```
crates/core (new in E4)    roots and paths, walk, small files, inspect and save (jackbird-tags),
                           streaming hashes; no Tauri, no HTTP
   ├── src-tauri (E4)      the desktop app: the window, Tauri commands as thin calls into core
   └── the service (later) its own binary: core behind HTTP (axum), serving the web build too
web  ── HTTP ──> the service
desktop app ── in process ──> core            (and, maybe, HTTP to the service: question 2)
```

- **The service** is a binary of its own, with no window. It serves the library over HTTP to the
  web (which it can serve itself, from the same origin, as F1 planned) and to other devices.
- **The desktop app** keeps its core in process, as E4 plans: it works with no service installed.
- **Installing it from the desktop app**: the app carries the service's binary (or fetches the
  release's), copies it to its place, and registers it the way each system starts things:
  - Linux: a systemd user unit (`~/.config/systemd/user/`, `systemctl --user enable --now`), no
    root; `loginctl enable-linger` if it must run with nobody logged in.
  - macOS: a launchd agent (`~/Library/LaunchAgents/`, `launchctl bootstrap gui/<uid>`), no admin;
    a system daemon would need admin.
  - Windows: a real Windows service needs administrator rights (an elevation prompt, the Service
    Control Manager); per user, a task at logon in the Task Scheduler needs none.
  The app also uninstalls it, and shows whether it runs.

### What it changes in E4 (proposed)

- **E4-D3, amended**: the native work (the roots registry and its path checks, `walk`, the small
  files, `inspect`, `save`, the streaming hashes) goes in a new crate, `crates/core`, with no
  Tauri dependency and no I/O of its own beyond the file system; `src-tauri/` only wraps it in
  commands. Its tests run in `cargo test --workspace`. The cost is little more than E4's plan
  already has; it is what lets the service reuse the core without moving code later.
- `AGENTS.md`'s rule of the crates ("they do no I/O") then holds for `crates/id3`, `crates/flac`,
  `crates/tags` and `crates/tags-wasm`; `crates/core` is the one crate that touches the file
  system, by design. Said so in the rule.
- Nothing else in E4 changes: the service, its HTTP API, its installer and its start-up are a
  step of their own after E4.

### Where it meets the family instance (F0–F5)

The service looks like F1's server run on the owner's own machine: one binary, `crates/tags`
natively, HTTP, the web build served from it, the instance modes of 21:35 (open or with sign-in,
one person or several). Question 1 asks whether they are the same program.

### Questions for the owner

1. Is the service the family instance's server (F1), the same binary installed on the desktop
   machine, or a smaller one (one person, local folders, no store of its own) that F1 grows from?
2. When the service is installed and running on the same machine, should the desktop app use it
   (as one more client) instead of its own core? Otherwise both would write the same folders'
   files and `.meta/` at once, which nothing serialises across processes: one of them would have
   to defer (a lock in the folder, or the app connecting to the service when it finds it).
3. Who reaches the service: only this machine (the web on `localhost`), the home network, or
   outside home too (intergate)? It decides its default mode (21:35: an open instance listens only
   on the machine and private networks unless forced) and whether sign-in comes with it.
4. Where it goes in the roadmap: right after E4 (before E5, E6 and F0), or merged with F1?

## Extension — 2026-11-08 14:14 — The service: the owner's answers; what they settle and what they open

**Summary**: The owner answered three of 13:55's four questions. The background service **is** the
family instance's backend (F1), one program. Sign-in is enabled from the interface and needs SMTP;
having several people is a separate switch; people may or may not be the system's own users. The
service and the desktop app may or may not share a machine. Remote access is by a tunnel to the
local server, or by an online server serving a local server's data. Some of this changes what
21:35 and 21:40 proposed; the open points are listed. Nothing is implemented; E4's plan (10:52,
with 13:55's `crates/core`) still awaits its three answers.

User message (translated): *"1. Yes, it's the same backend. From the interface it should be possible
to enable sign-in (it would need SMTP to be configured) and, independently, to add several users.
Users may or may not be users of the system. The service and the app may or may not live on the
same machine. Users may or may not be users of the system. 3. For example: a tunnel to the local
server; an online server serving remotely the data of a local server."*

### What it settles

- **One backend**: the service the desktop app installs is F1's server, the same binary, whether it
  runs beside the app or on a NAS or a VPS. `crates/core` (13:55) is its core too.
- **Two independent switches, in the interface**: sign-in on or off, one person or several. That is
  21:35's table (open or with sign-in, one or several), now set from the interface rather than only
  in the instance's configuration.
- **The desktop app is a client of any service**: on the same machine or another, it reaches it
  over HTTP as one more source (F1's `HttpAdapter`), and keeps its own core for local folders.
- **Remote access, two kinds of route**:
  - a tunnel to the local server (intergate or another; 21:40 keeps jackbird independent of it);
  - an online server serving a local server's data: an instance on the internet that relays, or
    mirrors, what the instance at home holds. New: until now every route reached the home instance
    itself.

### What it changes from earlier proposals

- **Sign-in needs SMTP**: 21:40 proposed passkeys, with a one-time code on the server's console for
  the first owner and for recovery, so that "no email is required". With SMTP, email becomes part
  of sign-in (question 1).
- **People who are the system's users**: until now people existed only in the service's database
  (21:32's roles). An option to tie them to the system's accounts is new (question 2).

### Open questions for the owner

1. **What SMTP is for**: invitations and recovery by email, with passkeys to sign in (21:40)? A
   link by email to sign in (no password, no passkey)? Passwords, with email to verify and reset
   them? And does the console code stay for the first owner, before SMTP is set up?
2. **"Users of the system"**: signing in with the machine's own account and password (PAM on
   Linux, the system's on macOS and Windows)? Or each person's files being their own home folder,
   read with their system permissions? The second needs the service to run with rights over other
   accounts' files (root, or one service per person).
3. **The service and the app on one machine, both on the same folder** (13:55, question 2, not yet
   answered): should the app use the service when it finds it, or should one of them defer through
   a lock in the folder?
4. **The online server serving a local server's data**: the same binary in a relay mode, which the
   home instance connects out to (so no port is opened at home), passing requests and streams
   through? Or a mirror, holding a copy (then it is the vfs-sync mesh, C4 and M3)?
5. **Where it goes in the roadmap** (13:55, question 4): right after E4, or merged with F1?

## Extension — 2026-11-08 14:57 — The service: sign-in, system users, the app beside it, the online route (the owner's answers)

**Summary**: The owner answered 14:14's questions 1 to 4. Sign-in has no passwords: email
one-time codes (by SMTP) to sign up and sign in, and passkeys to make signing in simple. Using the
system's users means the service runs as root. The desktop app is only an interface when the
service is installed, and its own backend otherwise. The online route is an outbound SSH
connection from the local server to the online one, as intergate does. Still open: where the
service goes in the roadmap (14:14, question 5), and E4's three questions (10:52).

User message (translated): *"1. For sending OTPs, sign-up and sign-in. We won't use passwords. To
make signing in simple we'll use passkeys. 2. Right, to use the system's users it would need to run
as root. 3. It works only as an interface when the service is installed; otherwise it uses its own
backend. 4. We can use an outbound SSH connection to the online server, as intergate does."*

### Settled

- **Sign-in** (supersedes 21:40's "no email is required"): no passwords. A person signs up and
  signs in with a one-time code sent by email (SMTP, set up from the interface when sign-in is
  turned on), and can register a passkey to sign in without the code afterwards; the code stays the
  way back in when a passkey is lost. 21:40's passkeys on the instance's own address stand.
  Assumed, unless the owner says otherwise: before SMTP is set up, the first owner claims the
  instance with 21:40's one-time code on the server's console.
- **System users**: an option of the service. Off, people exist only in its database (21:32's
  roles). On, a person is one of the machine's accounts and reaches the files that account can, so
  the service runs as root. That is a root process answering the network: when it is planned, it
  needs its own safeguards (each request's file work done with that person's rights, not root's;
  the option refused unless the service is reached only through sign-in), to be weighed then.
- **The desktop app and the service**: when the service is installed on the machine, the app is
  only its interface (a client over HTTP, as the web is) and does not open folders itself; when it
  is not, the app uses its own backend (`crates/core`, E4). So the two never write the same files
  at once (14:14, question 3, closed). To decide in the service's plan: what the app does when the
  service is installed but stopped (start it, or say so).
- **The online route**: the local server keeps an outbound SSH connection to an online server and
  forwards its port through it (a reverse tunnel, `ssh -R`, as intergate does), so nothing is
  opened at home; the online server receives HTTPS and passes it down the tunnel. jackbird stays
  independent of intergate (21:40): intergate is one such online server, any SSH server with a
  reverse proxy is another. To decide in the service's plan: whether the service opens and keeps
  the tunnel itself (set up from the interface) or the owner runs it beside it.

### Still open

1. Where the service goes in the roadmap: right after E4, or merged with F1 (14:14, question 5).
2. E4's three questions (10:52): media URLs, saves whose tags change size, signing.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 15:01 — Approved: E4 with its recommendations and crates/core; where the service goes (proposal)

**Summary**: The owner approved E4's plan (10:52) with its three recommendations (the asset
protocol on macOS and Windows and a local server on Linux; a save whose tags change size written
beside the old file and renamed over it; unsigned bundles) and 13:55's `crates/core`
(*"Ok"*). E4 starts. The owner asked for a recommendation on where the service goes in the
roadmap (*"Qué me recomiendas"*): proposed below, awaiting approval.

### Where the service goes (proposal)

The service is F1's backend (14:14), so it belongs to the family instance's steps, not beside
them. Proposed: the F steps come right after E4, before E5 and E6, and begin with the smallest
service that is useful, over the folders as they are, leaving F1's content-addressed store for
later:

| Step | Scope |
| --- | --- |
| S1 The service | `crates/core` behind HTTP (axum): the operations of E4-D2 and E4-D3 over local folders, ranged reads for playback, the web build served from it; one person, open, listening on the machine only; the web and the desktop app as its clients (the app only an interface when it is installed, 14:24) |
| S2 Install | from the desktop app: the binary put in place and registered the way each system starts things (a systemd user unit, a launchd agent, a Windows service or a task at logon), started, stopped, removed; the app's switch between its own backend and the service |
| S3 Sign-in and people | the two switches (sign-in, several people); SMTP set up from the interface; one-time codes by email to sign up and sign in; passkeys on the instance's own address; roles (21:32); F0's passkey questions answered first |
| S4 The online route | the outbound SSH connection to an online server (14:24), kept up by the service, set up from the interface |
| then F1–F5 as planned | the content-addressed store and metadata layers, each person's own data, concurrent edits and backups, the Docker image; system users (root, 14:24) weighed there |

Why this order:

- **Each step is usable on its own**: after S1 the web reaches the desktop machine's library;
  after S2 it runs without the app; after S3 the family can use it; after S4, from outside home.
- **It reuses E4's work at once**: S1 is `crates/core` behind HTTP instead of behind Tauri.
- **It helps E6**: a phone app that is a client of the service avoids most of the file-access
  problems the mobile spike is about (Android SAF, iOS bookmarks).
- **E5 (native output) is optional** and independent; it can go after.
- **The store can wait**: F1's import into a store by hashes is the largest single step, and
  everything above works on the folders as they are; it comes when the family's shared catalogue
  needs it.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 15:17 — E4, the desktop app: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: E4 as planned at 10:52, with 13:55's `crates/core` and the answers of 14:42 (the
asset protocol on macOS and Windows, a local server on Linux; a save whose tags change size
written beside and renamed over; unsigned bundles). The desktop app opens a folder, reads its
tags natively, saves, plays MP3 and FLAC, and reopens the folder after a restart: verified here on
Linux (WebKitGTK 2.52.6 under Xvfb), by the same unattended check that `desktop.yml` runs on the
three systems. The first CI run of `desktop.yml` was queued at the time of writing; its result,
for macOS and Windows, comes in the next extension. The web app behaves as before.

### What was done

1. **The seams in TypeScript** (E4-D1, E4-D2):
   - `RootSource` (`src/types.ts`): `vfs` (small files), `saved` (what the session keeps: a
     handle or a path), `walk`, `inspect(path, known?)`, `save(path, previous, metadata)`,
     `openMedia(path)`, `close()`. `RootWorkspace.rootHandle` became `source`.
   - `src/lib/platform.ts` (`isDesktop`); `src/lib/sources/index.ts` (`pickSource`,
     `restoreSource`), `browser.ts` (today's behaviour, moved: FSA, the wasm engine, playback
     from the file; `src/lib/media.ts` retired into it), `desktop.ts` (the app's commands; a
     `VFSAdapter` over them, small files' bytes sent raw with the root and path in
     percent-encoded headers; media URLs by `convertFileSrc` or the local server).
   - The scan, the verifier and the editor call `source.inspect` and `source.save`; the player
     calls `source.openMedia`; `closeRoot` calls `source.close()`. The session keeps
     `Session<FileSystemDirectoryHandle | string>` in the same IndexedDB store.
   - The `<audio>` element always has `crossOrigin = "anonymous"` (E4-D5).
2. **`crates/core`** (E4-D3, E4-D4), with no Tauri in it:
   - `Root`: `open`, `resolve` (relative paths only; no absolute path, no `..`; the deepest
     existing part resolved, links included, must stay under the root), `walk` (links not
     followed), `list`, `read`, `write` (a new file beside, then renamed over), `stat`,
     `delete`, `rename`, `mkdir`.
   - `inspect`: the layout from the head (64 KiB, grown as the engine asks, at least doubling)
     and the last 160 bytes; both SHA-1s in one pass of 1 MiB reads (`sha1` 0.10.6, its one
     dependency); the tags only when the full checksum is not the one known.
   - `save`: the engine's splice; in place when the new tags are as long as the old ones,
     otherwise a new file beside (the tags, then the old file's bytes after its tags, copied from
     disk; the permissions kept), its audio checked, renamed over the old one; after either, the
     audio region is hashed again and must be the source's. A refused save changes nothing.
   - Base64 for covers (the interface keeps data URLs).
3. **`src-tauri/`** (E4-D3, E4-D5, E4-D7): in the root workspace (edition 2024, the workspace's
   lints, `unsafe_code` still forbidden: Tauri's macros did not need an exception), depending
   on `jackbird-core` and `jackbird-tags`. Commands: `pick_folder` (the system's dialog, from
   Rust), `open_root`, `close_root`, `walk`, `list`, `read` (raw bytes back), `write` (raw bytes
   in), `stat`, `delete`, `rename`, `mkdir`, `inspect`, `save` (the edit made from the previous
   and new tags as `editFor` makes it in the browser), `media_url`, and the check's three. Errors
   reach the interface as a code and a message, shown as the browser shows the engine's.
   Media: the asset protocol, its scope given each root as it opens (no `**`), on macOS and
   Windows; on Linux `src-tauri/src/media.rs`, a server on 127.0.0.1 (a random port, a 128-bit
   token per run, only files the app handed out a numbered URL for, the last 256; byte ranges;
   CORS). `tauri.conf.json` builds from `../dist` (`npm run build`), develops over the dev server.
   E0's check page, its fixtures, `prepare.mjs` and `tauri-shell.yml` are gone; its findings
   stay in this record. `@tauri-apps/api` and `@tauri-apps/cli` 2.12.4, pinned.
4. **The unattended check and CI** (E4-D8): `src/lib/sources/check.ts`, loaded only in the app
   and acting only when the app is started for it, opens a folder through the desktop source,
   inspects every track, saves a title and reads it back, plays an MP3 and a FLAC three seconds
   through an analyser, opens the folder in the library as a person would, and reports; started
   again with `JACKBIRD_CHECK_RESTORE`, it reports what the session reopened.
   `scripts/desktop-check.mjs` runs both and judges the reports. `desktop.yml` builds the bundles
   on Ubuntu 22.04, macOS and Windows, runs the app's lints and tests (Linux), the check on each,
   and uploads the bundles; `release.yml` calls it and attaches the bundles to the release.
   `tags.yml` and `make test-crates` leave the app out (it needs WebKitGTK), `make desktop-test`
   checks it.
5. **Docs**: `README.md`, `docs/development.md` (the desktop app's requirements and commands),
   `docs/architecture.md` (the desktop app), `STACK.md`, `AGENTS.md` (`crates/core` the one crate
   on the file system; the desktop's checks), the Makefile.

### Checks

- `cargo fmt --check`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` (the app included,
  WebKitGTK installed here): clean. `cargo test --workspace --locked`: 96 pass. New:
  - `crates/core` (8): paths kept inside (`..`, absolute paths, `a/../b`, a link out refused and
    not walked); the small files written, read, listed, moved and deleted, no temporary file left;
    base64; `inspect` on every fixture giving its SHA-1, its region's SHA-1 from `cases.json` and
    the engine's tags, and no tags when the checksum is known; `save` on every fixture for three
    edits giving byte for byte what `jackbird_tags::write` gives on the whole file, both routes
    taken (in place and beside), a refused save leaving the file as it was, by its code; a 3 MB
    file hashed by chunks and saved.
  - The app (4): the edit's cover kept, set or removed as the browser's `editFor` does; headers
    percent-decoded; the media server's ranges, and that it serves only handed-out files (another
    number, the wrong token, a path: 404).
- `npm test`: 308 pass, the floors held. The web in Chromium (E2's and E3's scripts again): the
  same results as before E4.
- **The app on Linux** (release build, Xvfb, WebKitGTK 2.52.6), `scripts/desktop-check.mjs`: 26
  tracks found and read natively, titles as `cases.json` says (UTF-16, v2.2, FLAC included); a
  title saved into `mp3-v23-padded.mp3` and read back, the audio kept; the MP3 and the FLAC played
  from the local server (0.57 s and 0.5 s in, the analyser at 229 and 231); the library showed
  the folder's 26 tracks; started again, the session reopened it with its 26 tracks. A screenshot
  (`.tmp/e4/desktop-linux.png`, not committed) of the app started normally afterwards: the folder
  reopened from the session, two FLAC files added meanwhile found (28 tracks), covers drawn, the
  saved title shown.

### Left to the owner

- The bundles from CI, by hand on real machines (past the unsigned warnings): choose a folder in
  the dialog, hear a track, edit and save, quit and reopen.
- Not done in E4, by plan: the service and its installer (S1, S2), signing, automatic updates,
  watching folders, native output (E5), mobile (E6).

Suggested commit message: `feat(desktop): the Tauri v2 desktop app over crates/core; E4`.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 17:43 — Approved: the service's place in the roadmap; S1, the service over local folders: implementation plan

**Summary**: The owner approved 15:01's proposal (*"Ok a tu recomendación"*): the service's steps
S1 to S4 come right after E4, before E5, E6 and the rest of the family instance, whose F2 S3 takes
over. The roadmap gains `ROADMAP/2026-11-08/service.md`; `family-instance.md` and
`rust-tag-engine-and-tauri.md` point to it. Then the plan for S1: `crates/core` behind HTTP, the
web build served from it, one person, open, on the machine only. Six decisions are proposed;
three questions are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- `crates/core` has every operation the service needs (E4): roots and their path checks, `walk`,
  the small files, `inspect`, `save`. The desktop app wraps them in Tauri commands; the JSON it
  answers with (the tags as `TrackMetadata`, the inspection, the edit made from the previous and
  new tags) lives in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`.
- The web reaches a root through `RootSource` (E4): `browser.ts` (File System Access) and
  `desktop.ts` (the app's commands). A third kind, over HTTP, fits the same seam.
- The session keeps a handle (browser) or a path (desktop) in IndexedDB.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S1-D1 | **`crates/api`**: the JSON both wrappers speak (the tags as the interface keeps them, the inspection, the edit from previous and new tags, the error's code and message), moved out of `src-tauri/` with its tests; `serde` its one dependency. The desktop app and the service use it. | one shape on both sides, so the web's two clients of Rust (Tauri, HTTP) cannot drift |
| S1-D2 | **`crates/service`**, a binary `jackbird-service`: axum 0.8 on tokio, the operations of `crates/core` under `/api/` (roots: list, open, close; per root: walk, list, read, write, stat, delete, rename, mkdir, inspect, save) and `/api/media/…` for playback, with byte ranges (`tower-http`'s file service), file work off the async threads. A small configuration file in the system's configuration folder (`~/.config/jackbird/service.json`, `~/Library/Application Support/…`, `%APPDATA%\…`): the address, the roots it has open. | the core behind HTTP instead of behind Tauri, as 13:55 planned |
| S1-D3 | **The web served from it**: the web build embedded in the binary (`rust-embed`), served at `/`, so the page and the API share one origin (no CORS) and the binary is one file to install in S2. | one file for S2; no folder to keep beside it |
| S1-D4 | **Safe while open**: it listens on `127.0.0.1` only (and `::1`); every request must carry a `Host` of `localhost` or `127.0.0.1` with its port (a page on another site cannot reach it by renaming its own address to 127.0.0.1, "DNS rebinding"), and a request that changes anything must come from its own origin (`Origin` checked; no CORS), so another site open in the browser cannot write to the library. A different address is refused until S3 brings sign-in. | an open service is anyone's who reaches it (21:35); on the machine only, that is the person and their programs, not the web |
| S1-D5 | **The web's third source**, `src/lib/sources/service.ts`: when the page is served by the service (`platform.ts` asks `/api/info` once), roots are the service's, reached over HTTP; the media URL is the service's own. Choosing a folder: a dialog listing the service's machine's folders, from the person's home down (question 1). The session keeps the root's path, as on the desktop. | the views do not change; the same `dist/` serves the browser, the desktop app and the service |
| S1-D6 | **Builds and checks**: `service.yml` builds the binary on Linux, macOS and Windows, runs the crate's tests, and an unattended check: the service started on a copy of the fixtures, Chromium (Playwright) on its page opening the folder, reading the tags, saving a title, playing an MP3 and a FLAC, then the service restarted and the page reloaded, the folder back. The binaries are the run's artifacts; a release attaches them. | the step's "done when", on the three systems |

### Tests

- `cargo test`: `crates/api` (moved, with the desktop's tests); `crates/service` on a temporary
  folder through the router itself (no socket): every operation, paths that leave a root refused,
  ranged media reads, the `Host` and `Origin` checks (another host, another origin writing, a
  request with no origin writing: refused), the web build served.
- `npm test`: `service.ts`'s pure parts at the floors.
- By hand here and in CI: S1-D6's check.

### Steps (estimates)

1. `crates/api`, the desktop app on it (0.5 d).
2. `crates/service`: the router, the configuration, the roots, the operations, media, the guards,
   the embedded web; tests (2.5 d).
3. The web's service source and its folder dialog; `platform.ts` (1.5 d).
4. `service.yml`, the unattended check, the release's binaries (1.5 d).
5. Docs, the walkthrough, the roadmap (0.5 d).

### Done when

S1's row: the web, served by the service, opens a folder on the machine, plays, edits and reopens
it after a restart of the service, on the three systems.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Which folders the service may open**: any folder the system lets it read, chosen in a dialog
   that starts at the person's home and can go anywhere (recommended for S1: it runs as the
   person, on the machine only, so it reaches what they can already reach), or only folders
   under the home folder, or only folders listed in its configuration file?
2. **Its address**: `127.0.0.1` on a fixed default port, `4646` (recommended: easy to remember
   and to bookmark; changed in the configuration if taken), or a port the system picks, written
   to the configuration for the desktop app to find?
3. **How it starts in S1**, before S2 installs it: by hand from a terminal
   (`jackbird-service`, recommended: S2 is where the system starts it), or also a `--open` switch
   that opens the browser on its page?

## Extension — 2026-11-08 18:50 — S1, the service over local folders: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: S1 as planned at 17:43 and approved at 17:48. `jackbird-service` serves the web from
the same origin and puts `crates/core` behind HTTP; the web, served by it, opens a folder of the
machine through its own dialog, reads the tags, saves, plays, and finds the folder again after the
service restarts. Verified here on Linux in Chromium by the unattended check `service.yml` runs on
the three systems; CI's first run is pending at the time of writing and comes in the next
extension. The desktop app and the browser on its own behave as before.

### What was done

1. **`crates/api`** (S1-D1): the JSON of E4's desktop commands moved out of `src-tauri/`
   (`Failure`, `Opened`, `StatJson`, `EntryJson`, `Metadata`, `InspectionJson`, `cover_of`,
   `edit_for`) with its test; the desktop app uses it, as the service does.
2. **`crates/service`** (S1-D2 to S1-D4): `jackbird-service [--port N] [--config PATH]`.
   - axum 0.8.9 on tokio 1.54.1; `/api/info`, `/api/roots` (list, open; `/api/roots/close`),
     `/api/folders` (a folder of the machine and its subfolders, hidden ones left out, from the
     person's home), and per root `walk`, `list`, `read`, `write`, `stat`, `delete`, `mkdir`,
     `rename`, `inspect`, `save`; `/api/media` with byte ranges (`tower-http`'s `ServeFile`).
     File work runs off the async threads. Bodies up to 512 MB (`.meta/` holds covers).
   - The configuration (`service.json`: the port, 4646 by default, and the open folders) in the
     system's configuration folder, or `JACKBIRD_SERVICE_CONFIG`; written through a new file
     renamed over; the folders reopened when the service starts.
   - The web build embedded in a release binary (`rust-embed`, read from `dist/` in a debug one,
     a plain message when it is missing); any other path gets the page.
   - The guards: it listens on 127.0.0.1 and ::1 only; `Host` must be `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or
     `[::1]` with its port; `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site` and a foreign `Origin` are refused; a
     request that changes anything must carry its own `Origin`.
3. **The web** (S1-D5): `platform.ts`'s `isService()` (asks `/api/info` once);
   `src/lib/sources/service.ts` (the source over HTTP, the session keeping the path);
   `src/lib/folders.ts` and `src/views/folder-dialog.html` (the machine's folders: into one, up,
   "Open this folder"; texts `folders.*` in English and Spanish); `sources/index.ts` picks the
   service's dialog and restores its paths when the page is served by it.
4. **Builds and checks** (S1-D6): `scripts/service-check.cjs` (the service started with a home of
   its own holding the fixtures; Chromium opens the folder through the dialog, saves a title
   through the edit dialog, plays two tracks, which the service must serve, and a long FLAC when
   one is given, whose position must move; then the service is restarted, the page reloaded, and
   the saved title must show). `service.yml` runs the crates' tests, the web build, the release
   binary, the check (Playwright 1.63.0 installed for it; a 20 s FLAC made with ffmpeg on Linux),
   and uploads the binary named for its system; `release.yml` attaches them. `npm run service`,
   `make service`.
5. **Docs**: `README.md`, `docs/development.md` and `docs/architecture.md` (the service),
   `STACK.md`, `AGENTS.md`.

### Checks

- `cargo fmt --check`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings`: clean.
  `cargo test --workspace --locked`: 101 pass. New, in `crates/service` through its router:
  the guards (another `Host`, another port, a foreign `Origin` reading or writing,
  `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site`, a change with no `Origin`: refused; `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]`
  answered); roots opened, kept in the configuration, back after a restart, closed; every
  operation on the fixtures (a title saved with the audio kept, an invalid year refused by code,
  a 3 MB small file written and read, a rename into a new folder, paths leaving the root
  refused); media whole and by range (206, `Content-Range`), a path outside refused; the
  machine's folders listed.
- `npm test`: 308 pass, the floors held.

  20 s FLAC: the library showed 27 tracks; the save said "Metadata saved to flac-full.flac."; the

`jackbird-service` by hand from CI's binaries: start it, open <http://localhost:4646>, choose a

## Extension — 2026-11-08 18:42 — S2, installing the service from the desktop app: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for S2 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-08/service.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Adelante con S2"*): the desktop app carries the service's binary, installs it for the person
(no administrator rights on any system), starts, stops and removes it, and while it is installed
is only its interface. Seven decisions are proposed; four questions are left to the owner. Nothing
is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- S1's `jackbird-service` runs by hand; its configuration (port 4646, open folders) is in the
  system's configuration folder. Its guards accept requests from its own page only.
- The desktop app (E4) has its own backend (`crates/core` in process) and its root source
  `desktop.ts`; the web's `service.ts` speaks the service's HTTP from a page it serves.
- 14:57 settled: when the service is installed, the app is only its interface; otherwise it uses
  its own backend. 13:55 listed how each system starts things for a person.
- This container has no systemd (no user bus), so the Linux registration cannot run here: it is
  tested by its generated files here and for real in CI.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S2-D1 | **The service installs itself**: `jackbird-service install`, `uninstall`, `start`, `stop`, `status` (a line of JSON: installed, running, its version, its address). The desktop app runs those from its own copy of the binary; the same commands work from a terminal (a NAS, a machine with no desktop). What each system needs (the files, the commands) is generated by pure functions in `crates/service`, tested on every system. | one place knows each system's start-up; the app only asks |
| S2-D2 | **Per person, no administrator rights**: the binary copied to the person's own data folder (`~/.local/share/jackbird/bin/`, `~/Library/Application Support/jackbird/bin/`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\jackbird\bin\`), so moving or updating the app does not break it; registered as **a systemd user unit** (`~/.config/systemd/user/jackbird.service`, `systemctl --user enable --now`) on Linux, **a launchd agent** (`~/Library/LaunchAgents/es.germade.jackbird.service.plist`, `launchctl bootstrap gui/<uid>`) on macOS, **a task at logon in the Task Scheduler** (`schtasks`, the person's own, limited rights) on Windows (question 1). Each restarts it if it stops. Uninstalling stops it, unregisters it and removes the binary; the configuration (the open folders) stays unless asked. | the service runs as the person, so it reaches what they can (S1's choice), and installing asks for nothing |
| S2-D3 | **The app carries the binary** as a Tauri sidecar (`externalBin`): `desktop.yml` builds `jackbird-service` for the same system first, named for its target, and the bundle includes it. | one download installs both; the versions match |
| S2-D4 | **The app as an interface of the service**: when `status` says it is installed, the app's roots are the service's, through `service.ts` with the service's address as its base (`http://localhost:4646`), instead of `desktop.ts`. The service's guards accept, besides its own page, the desktop app's page origins (`tauri://localhost`, `http://tauri.localhost`), answering them with CORS for those origins only; every other origin stays refused. | the same client the web uses; no second path in Rust |
| S2-D5 | **Installed but stopped**: the app starts it (`jackbird-service start`) and, if it does not answer within a few seconds, says so and offers to uninstall it, rather than falling back to its own backend behind the person's back (two writers). | 13:55's open point; one writer at a time |
| S2-D6 | **Updates**: when the app's copy is newer than the installed one (`/api/info`'s version), the app replaces it (stop, copy, start) on start, and says so (question 3). | the interface and the service from one release, so `crates/api` matches |
| S2-D7 | **In the interface**: the preferences dialog, in the desktop app only, gets a "Service" section: its state (not installed; installed and running at its address; installed and stopped), and Install, Start, Uninstall; while it is installed, a line saying folders are opened by the service. Texts in English and Spanish. | the owner's "from the desktop interface there would be an option to install the service" |

### Not in S2

Sign-in and other people (S3), reaching it from other machines (it keeps listening on the
machine only), the online route (S4), a system-wide service running as root (system users, 14:57),
signing the binaries.

### Tests and checks

- `cargo test`: the generated unit, plist and task (paths quoted, the binary's place, the
  restart rule); `status` on a machine where nothing is installed; the guards with the desktop
  app's origins (answered, with CORS for that origin only) and others (refused).
- `npm test`: the floors.
- CI, on the three systems (`desktop.yml`): `jackbird-service install`, `status` (running, the
  version), the app's unattended check run against the installed service (the app only an
  interface: the folder opened, read, saved and played through it, and back after a restart),
  then `uninstall` and `status` (not installed, nothing left but the configuration). On Linux the
  runner's user manager is started for it (`loginctl enable-linger`), since CI has no login session.
- Here: the generated files, the CLI on a machine with nothing installed, the desktop app against a
  service started by hand (the app as an interface) under Xvfb. The owner, by hand: install from
  the app on each system, log out and in, find it running.

### Steps (estimates)

1. The commands and their generated files in `crates/service`; tests (1.5 d).
2. The sidecar in the bundle; `desktop.yml` builds the service first (0.5 d).
3. The app's switch: `status` on start, `service.ts` with a base address, the guards' desktop
   origins, starting a stopped one, replacing an older one (1.5 d).
4. The preferences' "Service" section (0.5 d).
5. CI's install, check and uninstall on the three systems; the docs; the walkthrough (1 d).

### Done when

S2's row: the desktop app installs the service, uses it, and removes it, on the three systems.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Windows**: a task at logon in the Task Scheduler (recommended: no administrator rights, runs
   as the person, so it reaches their folders; it starts when they log in), or a real Windows
   service (an administrator prompt; it starts before anyone logs in, but runs as a system account
   that does not reach the person's folders unless it is given their password)?
2. **Linux without systemd** (some distributions): refuse to install, saying why (recommended),
   or fall back to starting it when the person logs in to their desktop (an XDG autostart entry),
   which does not restart it if it stops?
3. **Updates**: the app replaces an older installed service on its own and says so (recommended),
   or asks first?
4. **Start at boot without logging in**: not in S2 (recommended: everything per person; Linux's
   `enable-linger` would allow it, macOS and Windows would need administrator rights), or as an
   option where the system allows it without administrator rights (Linux only)?

## Extension — 2026-11-08 18:51 — S2: the owner's answers; two ways to install on every system (proposal)

**Summary**: The owner answered S2's four questions (18:42). On Windows both ways are offered,
starting at logon or installed as a service; Linux requires systemd and says so; updates are a
notification in the app, not a silent replacement; and the desktop app must not need
administrator rights to work. Since questions 1 and 4 meet, the proposal below offers the same
two ways on the three systems. Two points are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented yet.

User message (translated): *"1. I'd offer both options, start at logon or install as a service.
2. OK, it says systemd is required. 3. I'd show a notification in the app saying when there are
updates. 4. In what scenario? The desktop app should not need root to work."*

### Question 4: the scenario

A machine that serves the music when nobody is logged in to it: a computer at home left on, or
restarted after an update or a power cut, which the web (or, later, a phone, S3 and S4) should
reach without someone logging in first. Per person, the service starts only when its person logs
in. The desktop app never needs administrator rights to work: its own backend, and the service
started at logon, need none; only installing the service to start with the system asks for them,
once, through the system's own prompt, and only if the person chooses it.

### Two ways to install, on every system (amends S2-D2)

| | **At logon** (the default) | **With the system** (optional) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When it runs | from when the person logs in | from boot, before anyone logs in |
| Runs as | the person | the person (not root, not a system account) |
| Administrator rights | none | once, to install and to remove (the system's own prompt) |
| Linux | a systemd user unit | a systemd system unit with `User=` the person (`pkexec` asks) |
| macOS | a launchd agent | a launchd daemon with `UserName` the person (the system's authorisation dialog) |
| Windows | a task at logon (Task Scheduler) | a Windows service running as the person (UAC asks; Windows needs the person's password to run a service as them: see question 1) |

Either way it reaches what the person can reach, as S1 chose, and no more: not root, so 14:57's
system users stay a decision for later. Uninstalling removes whichever is installed.

### Linux without systemd (amends S2-D2)

Installing says that systemd is required, and installs nothing.

### Updates (replaces S2-D6)

When the app's own copy of the service is newer than the installed one (`/api/info`'s
version), the app shows a notification ("A newer service is available") with an Update key:
stop, copy, start, through the same way it was installed (asking for administrator rights again
if it was installed with the system). Nothing is replaced unasked.

### Open points for the owner

1. **The Windows service and the person's password**: Windows runs a service as a given person
   only with that person's password, which it keeps itself (as the Services console does). The
   app would ask for it once in its own form and hand it to Windows (`sc.exe`), never keeping it.
   The alternative, a system account, reaches every file on the machine, as root would. Proposed:
   ask for the password; with a passwordless Windows account (a PIN or Windows Hello only), the
   "with the system" way is not offered and says why.
2. **"When there are updates"**: only the installed service against the app's own copy (proposed
   for S2), or also new releases of jackbird published on GitHub, checked online by the app? The
   second is the app's own updates, kept out of E4 and S2 until now; a step of its own.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 19:10 — S2, installing the service from the desktop app: walkthrough (the app side verified here; installing verified in CI)

**Summary**: S2 as planned at 18:42, amended at 18:57 and approved. `jackbird-service` installs,
updates, starts, stops and uninstalls itself, at logon or with the system, on the three systems,
always running as the person; the desktop app carries it, offers it in its Preferences, and while
it is installed is only its interface. Here (no systemd in this container) the app as the
service's interface was verified under Xvfb against a service started by hand, and installing was
verified to refuse without systemd; real installation on the three systems is in `desktop.yml`,
whose first run with it is pending and comes in the next extension.

### What was done

1. **`crates/service/src/install.rs`** (S2-D1, S2-D2 as amended): pure plans for each system and
   way (the binary's place, the files, the commands), and their running:
   - at logon: a systemd user unit (`~/.config/systemd/user/jackbird.service`, restarted on
     failure), a launchd agent (`~/Library/LaunchAgents/es.germade.jackbird.service.plist`, kept
     alive), a Task Scheduler task at the person's logon (least privilege, no time limit, restarted
     on failure; its XML written as UTF-16); the binary in the person's own data folder;
   - with the system: a systemd system unit with `User=`, a launchd daemon with `UserName`, a
     Windows service logged on as the person (`sc.exe create … obj= .\person password= …`, the
     password read from a file the elevated command removes, written nowhere else); the binary in a
     folder of the system's. The binary runs under the Service Control Manager when started so
     (`windows-service` 0.8.1, Windows only);
   - `installed`, `install` (refused when installed already, and on Linux without systemd: "needs
     systemd"), `uninstall` (the configuration stays), `update` (the binary written beside and
     renamed over, then restarted), `start`, `stop`.
2. **The binary's commands**: `install [--with-system] [--user --uid --home --config]
   [--password-file]`, `uninstall`, `update`, `start`, `stop`, `status` (one line of JSON:
   installed and how, running, its version, its port, this binary's version), `--version`. Run as
   an administrator for a person (`--home`), it uses their configuration, not root's. Its version
   is jackbird's (`package.json`, read by `build.rs`), so a release's app and service compare.
3. **The guards** (S2-D4): the desktop app's page origins (`tauri://localhost`,
   `http://tauri.localhost`, `https://tauri.localhost`) are answered, with CORS for that origin
   alone (preflights included); every other origin stays refused.
4. **The desktop app** (S2-D3 to S2-D7):
   - `scripts/sidecar.mjs` builds the service and puts it where `externalBin` takes it;
     `tauri.conf.json` runs it before every build; the bundle carries it beside the app.
   - `src-tauri/src/service.rs`: `service_status`, `service_install` (plain at logon; with the
     system through `pkexec`, `osascript … with administrator privileges`, or PowerShell's
     `Start-Process -Verb RunAs`), `service_uninstall`, `service_update`, `service_start` (each
     elevated when it is installed with the system), `choose_folder` (the dialog, its path only).
   - `src/lib/desktop-service.ts`: when it is installed, the app's roots are the service's
     (`service.ts` with the service's address as its base), a stopped one is started (or the
     library says it does not answer), and an installed service older than the app's copy is
     announced ("A newer service is available: update it in Preferences"). Installing or
     uninstalling reloads the window, so every root is opened again by its new backend.
   - Preferences, in the app only: the service's state; Install, Install to start with the system
     (on Windows, the password field first; the key waits for it), Start, Update, Uninstall; texts
     in English and Spanish. `src/helpers/version.ts` compares versions (tested).
   - The unattended check opens its folder through whichever backend the app uses
     (`restoreSource`), and `desktop-check.mjs` checks which (`JACKBIRD_CHECK_KIND`).
5. **CI** (`desktop.yml`): the sidecar built first; after the app's check, on each system: the
   service installed at logon from the app's copy, running, the app's check run again with the app
   as its interface, uninstalled; then, on Linux and macOS, installed with the system (`sudo`, for
   the runner's user), running, uninstalled. On Linux the runner's user manager is started first
   (`loginctl enable-linger`).
6. **Docs**: `README.md`, `docs/development.md`, `docs/architecture.md`; the Makefile.

### Departures from the plan, noted

- A passwordless Windows account is not detected ahead: the "with the system" key waits for a
  password, and Windows refuses a wrong or missing one, which the app shows.
- Windows' "with the system" way is not run in CI: a runner's account has no password to give.
  It is left to the owner, by hand.

### Checks

- `cargo test --workspace --locked`: 111 pass (new: each system's plans for both ways, quoting
  and escaping, the restart after an update, a binary replaced whole, nothing installed on a clean
  home; the desktop app's origins answered with CORS for themselves alone, look-alikes refused; the
  app's shell quoting). `cargo clippy -D warnings`: clean, natively and for Windows
  (`x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, `cargo check` and `clippy` of the service, the Windows service code
  included); macOS (`x86_64-apple-darwin`) checked too.
- `npm test`: 311 pass, the floors held (20 helpers).
- Here, under Xvfb (WebKitGTK): the app's check with its own backend passes; with a service
  started by hand and its unit file standing in for an installation, the app's check passes as its
  interface (the roots `service`, the tracks read, a title saved with the audio kept, MP3 and FLAC
  played from the service with CORS, the folder back after a restart). Preferences show the
  section; Install there says, in place, that systemd is required. The service's Chromium check
  passes as before.

Suggested commit message: `feat(desktop): install the service from the app, at logon or with the system; the app as its interface; S2`.

## Extension — 2026-11-08 19:29 — S2 done: the service installed and removed by CI on the three systems

**Summary**: The owner opened pull request
[#11](https://github.com/jgermade/jackbird/pull/11) for this branch and asked CI to be watched
(*"Vigila el CI"*). The first run with S2 (commit `e2b8b58`) failed twice, both in the tests and
the checks, not in the service: fixed in `ab5582e`, on which every workflow passes, on push and
on the pull request. S2's "done when" is met as far as machines without a person can tell; the
roadmap marks it done.

### The two failures, and their fixes (`ab5582e`)

- `service.yml` on Windows: two tests of the Linux and macOS plans compared paths written with
  `/`, while `PathBuf::join` on Windows joins with `\`. The plans are right (they run only on
  their own system); the tests now build what they expect as the code does.
- `desktop.yml` on macOS: after installing at logon and finding it running (`{"installed":"logon",
  "running":true}`), the check's line failed in the script itself: macOS's bash 3.2 calls an empty
  array "unbound" under `set -u`. Written as `${run[@]+"${run[@]}"}`.

### What passed (`ab5582e`)

- `desktop.yml` ([run 42514407389](https://github.com/jgermade/jackbird/actions/runs/42514407389),
  and [42514412426](https://github.com/jgermade/jackbird/actions/runs/42514412426) on the pull
  request), on Linux, macOS and Windows: the app's own check; then the service installed at logon
  from the app's copy (a systemd user unit, a launchd agent, a task at logon), running, the app's
  check again with the app as its interface (its roots the service's), uninstalled and gone; then,
  on Linux and macOS, installed with the system for the runner's user (a systemd system unit with

## Extension — 2026-11-09 10:45 — S3, sign-in and people: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for S3 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-08/service.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Seguimos?"*, on a new branch from `main` after #11): the service's two switches (sign-in,
several people) set from the interface; SMTP set up from it; one-time codes by email to sign up
and sign in; passkeys on the instance's own address; the four roles of 21:32 (2026-11-04) enforced
by the service; the first owner's claim and a console code for recovery. Ten decisions are
proposed; five questions are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- S1 and S2: `jackbird-service` is open, for one person, on the machine only. Its guard
  (`crates/service/src/lib.rs`) accepts its own loopback names as `Host`, its own page and the
  desktop app's page as `Origin`; there are no people, sessions or roles. Its configuration
  (`service.json`) holds the port and the open folders.
- `/api/folders` lets its page browse the machine's folders from the person's home, which is
  fine while only that person reaches it, and is the first thing sign-in must guard.
- The owner's answers (2026-11-08, 14:14 and 14:57): no passwords; codes by email to sign up and
  sign in; passkeys to sign in without the code, the code staying the way back in; sign-in and
  several people as two independent switches, in the interface. 21:40 (2026-11-04): the
  passkeys' RP ID is the instance's own address, a setting. 21:35: an open instance listens on
  the machine and private networks only. Assumed at 14:57, to confirm (question 4): the first
  owner's console code before SMTP is set up.
- Per-person data (favourites, playlists, their own metadata) is F3's, and the store F1's: in S3
  everyone sees the same folders, and a role decides what they may do with them.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S3-D1 | **A database for people**: `jackbird.db`, SQLite (`rusqlite`, bundled, so nothing to install), beside `service.json`: people (email, name, role), pending codes, sessions, passkeys, invitations, and the log of changes. The instance's settings (the switches, its address, SMTP) stay in `service.json`, written owner-only (`0600`; the profile's own ACL on Windows); the SMTP password is never sent back to the interface (a field you can replace, not read). | F1 plans SQLite; S3 starts it with the tables only people need |
| S3-D2 | **The two switches** (21:35's table): sign-in off and one person is S1 as it is; sign-in off and several people is profiles to pick in the browser, with roles as defaults, not protection (question 5); sign-in on and one person is the owner alone, no invitations; sign-in on and several people is the family instance. With sign-in off the service keeps listening on the machine only, whatever the other switch says. | the owner's two switches; an open service is anyone's who reaches it |
| S3-D3 | **Turning sign-in on, and the first owner**: from Preferences, on the machine (so by the person the open service already answers): SMTP's host, port, security (TLS or STARTTLS), user and password; their own email; a code sent there and typed back, which proves SMTP works and makes them the owner; then sign-in is on. Without a screen (a NAS) or with SMTP broken: `jackbird-service owner-code <email>`, run on the machine as the service's person, prints a one-time code (15 minutes) that signs in as an owner, creating it if there is none (question 4). Turning sign-in off again is an owner's, from the machine. | no one is locked out; a code on the console proves access to the machine, as 21:40 proposed |
| S3-D4 | **One-time codes**: 6 digits, valid 10 minutes, used once, 5 tries; kept as an HMAC (a key of the instance's own), never in clear; at most one a minute and ten an hour per address; the answer is the same whether the address is known or not, so no one learns who has an account. The email in the person's language (English, Spanish), sent by `lettre` over rustls, off the async threads. | the owner's "OTP to sign up and sign in"; the usual guards against guessing and probing |
| S3-D5 | **Sign-up by invitation** (question 1): an owner or admin adds an email with a role; the invitation is an email with a link (valid 7 days); the person opens it, gets a code at that address, types it, and is in. With "one person" there are no invitations. | 21:32's "invite people"; a family instance is not open to strangers |
| S3-D6 | **Passkeys**: after signing in, "Add a passkey" (named by the person, several allowed; listed, renamed, removed). The RP ID is the host of the instance's address (S3-D7); passkeys are offered only where the browser allows them, in a secure context (HTTPS, or `localhost`), and the code is always there. Verified by jackbird's own code on RustCrypto (`p256` for ES256, `ed25519-dalek`, `rsa` for RS256; `ciborium` for CBOR), "none" attestation only, sign counts checked (question 3). | the owner's passkeys; no OpenSSL to build on three systems |
| S3-D7 | **The instance's address and who reaches it**: a setting, its public origin (`https://music.example.com`), from which the RP ID comes and which the `Host` and `Origin` guards accept besides the loopback names. With sign-in on, an owner may also open it to the home network (listening on every address; the guards then accept the machine's own addresses and its `.local` name); over plain HTTP there the interface says that codes and the session travel unencrypted and that passkeys are not available (question 2). HTTPS comes from the route: a reverse proxy in front, or S4's online server. | the address does not change when the route does (21:40) |
| S3-D8 | **Sessions**: a cookie (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, `Secure` over HTTPS), 32 random bytes kept hashed, valid 30 days from its last use; signing out ends it; "My account" lists a person's sessions (browser, last seen) and ends any of them. The desktop app, a page of another origin, signs in through the same form and gets a token sent as `Authorization: Bearer`, kept by the app; its media URLs carry a short-lived token for that one file (an `<audio>` sends no header). | one session per device, revocable; the app's page cannot share the service's cookie |
| S3-D9 | **Roles, enforced by the service** (21:32), checked on every route: **listeners** read (walk, list, read, stat, inspect, media); **editors** also write (save tags and covers, write, rename, mkdir, delete, playlists); **admins** also invite, change roles up to editor, remove listeners and editors, read the log, and set SMTP; **owners** also open and close folders, browse the machine's folders (`/api/folders`), set the switches and the address, and make and unmake admins and owners, with at least one owner always left. Every change to the folders is logged (who, when, what, where). The interface hides what a role may not do; the service is what refuses it. For listeners the interface keeps its snapshot cache (`.meta/`) in the browser only and shows playlists read-only until F3 gives them their own. | 21:32's roles; the machine's folders only for owners |
| S3-D10 | **In the interface**: a sign-in page (email, then the code; "Sign in with a passkey"); Preferences gains "Sign-in and people" (the switches, SMTP with a test email, the address, people and invitations, the log) for those whose role allows it, and "My account" (name, email, passkeys, sessions, sign out) for everyone. Texts in English and Spanish. | the owner's "from the interface" |

### Not in S3

Each person's own data and metadata (F3), the store (F1), the online route (S4), system users
(the service as root, 14:57), changing a person's email (a code to the new address; later),
TLS served by the service itself (unless question 2 says otherwise), passkeys inside the desktop
app's window (its webviews differ; the app signs in by code, and passkeys can come later).

### Tests and checks

- `cargo test`, through the router (no socket): every route against every role and against no
  session (a table, so a new route cannot be added without its role); codes (expired, reused, the
  sixth try, the rate limits, the same answer for unknown addresses); invitations (expired, used,
  their role); sessions (expired, revoked, bearer and cookie); the last owner kept; the guards with
  the instance's address and the home network; passkeys registered and asserted by a software
  authenticator built in the tests (ES256 and Ed25519; a replayed assertion, a wrong RP ID, a wrong
  origin and a lower sign count refused); SMTP against a small SMTP listener in the tests.
- `npm test`: the new helpers (the sign-in form's states, what a role shows) at the floors.
- CI (`service.yml`, the three systems): an unattended check with a local SMTP sink and Chromium's
  virtual authenticator: sign-in turned on from the machine, the owner claims it by the emailed
  code, invites an editor and a listener; each signs in (the editor with a passkey added after the
  first code); the editor saves a title, the listener's save and folder browsing are refused by the
  service and not offered by the interface; the log shows the editor's save; the service
  restarted, the sessions kept.

### Steps (estimates)

1. `jackbird.db`, people, codes, sessions, invitations, the log; `owner-code`; tests (2 d).
2. SMTP: settings, sending, the test email; the codes' emails in two languages (1 d).
3. Roles on every route; the guards with the address and the home network; the desktop app's
   bearer and media tokens (1.5 d).
4. Passkeys: the verifier, registration and sign-in routes; tests with a software authenticator
   (1.5 d).
5. The interface: the sign-in page, "Sign-in and people", "My account", what each role shows;
   profiles if question 5 keeps them (1.5 d).
6. CI's check, the docs, the walkthrough, the roadmap (0.5 d).

### Done when

S3's row: with sign-in on, two people with different roles each do what the role allows and
nothing more.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Sign-up**: by invitation only, an owner or admin adding the email with its role
   (recommended), or also open sign-up, anyone with an email asking and an admin approving?
2. **The home network without HTTPS**: allowed, off by default, with the warning of S3-D7
   (recommended: on a home network it is how most people would reach it at first; HTTPS comes
   with a proxy or S4), or the service serving HTTPS itself with a certificate of its own (every
   browser warns until it is trusted on each device), or nothing but the machine and S4's route?
3. **Verifying passkeys**: jackbird's own verifier on RustCrypto, "none" attestation only
   (recommended: about 500 lines, pure Rust on the three systems, in line with E1's own tag
   crates), or `webauthn-rs`, which needs OpenSSL built on each system?
4. **The first owner** (14:57's assumption): made from the interface on the machine, by a code
   emailed to them that also proves SMTP works, with `jackbird-service owner-code` on the
   machine's console for a machine without a screen and for recovery (recommended), or only by
   the console code, as 21:40 first proposed?
5. **Several people without sign-in** (profiles to pick, roles as defaults, no protection):
   in S3, small, since the switches are independent (recommended), or left to F3, when each person
   has data of their own and a profile means something?
