## Extension — 2026-11-09 11:20 — S3: the owner's answers; intergate's way of signing in (proposal)

**Summary**: The owner answered S3's questions (10:45). Sign-up by invitation only; no
certificate of the service's own, a reverse proxy (Caddy, nginx) in front gives HTTPS; for
passkeys, look at how intergate does it; the first owner by email and a one-time code, from the
console or from the interface; question 5 to be explained with examples. intergate (read from
`jgermade/intergate`, `internal/web/`) is read below, and S3's decisions are amended to follow it.
Two points remain for the owner. Nothing is implemented yet.

User message (translated): *"1. Invitation only. 2. For now no certificate; I'll put a proxy in
front that handles it (caddy, nginx, etc). 3. Have a look at how intergate does it. 4. By email +
OTP, from the console or from the UI. 5. Explain it better, with examples."*

### How intergate signs people in

- **Passkeys** (`internal/web/passkeys.go`, `ui/lib/passkeys.js`): a library, `go-webauthn`, not
  its own verifier. Its choices: the RP ID is the instance's domain, and assertions are accepted
  from its exact origin only; passkeys are discoverable (resident key required) and user
  verification is required, so signing in needs no email first; the ceremony's state travels in a
  signed cookie valid 5 minutes, and each challenge is answered once; a credential is stored whole
  (the library's JSON) and updated on each use; a sign counter that goes backwards is refused as a
  cloned authenticator; passkeys are named (64 characters); after a sign-in by code with no
  passkey yet, the account page suggests adding one; the browser offers passkeys in the email
  field's autofill (conditional mediation). Its end-to-end tests use a software authenticator.
- **Codes by email** (`internal/web/email.go`): six digits; the code is bound to the browser that
  asked for it (a signed cookie carries the attempt's id, and the code is hashed with it); kept in
  memory, so a restart means asking again; 5 tries; at most 5 codes per address in 15 minutes and
  20 per IP address in an hour.
- **The first owner** (`internal/web/setup.go`, `internal/wizard/`): while nothing can send codes,
  or there is no owner, the server is in setup. `intergate setup-url` (or the server's log) gives a
  one-time link; only the browser that opened it can do the setup: SMTP (with its password never
  shown again), then the owner's address, confirmed by a code sent there. A console wizard sets the
  same settings without a browser.

### S3's decisions, amended

- **S3-D3, the first owner** (question 4): by email and a code, two ways, as intergate:
  - **from the interface**: on the machine, while the service is open, Preferences → Sign-in:
    SMTP, a test email, the owner's address, the code sent there typed back; then sign-in is on;
  - **from the console**: `jackbird-service setup`, run on the machine as the service's person,
    asks for SMTP and the owner's address and sends the code, which is typed back in the console;
    or `jackbird-service setup-url` prints a one-time link (valid 15 minutes) for a browser
    elsewhere to do the interface's setup (a machine without a screen).
  The same commands are the way back in for an owner (another owner's address, or SMTP fixed).
  No code is printed on the console: every code goes by email (replaces 21:40's console code).
- **S3-D4, codes**: intergate's rules: six digits, 10 minutes, bound to the browser that asked
  (a signed cookie), hashed with the attempt's id, kept in memory, 5 tries, 5 per address in
  15 minutes and 20 per IP address in an hour; the same answer for unknown addresses.
- **S3-D5, sign-up** (question 1): by invitation only, as proposed.
- **S3-D6, passkeys** (question 3): intergate's choices above (discoverable, user verification
  required, the exact origin, the signed ceremony cookie, single-use challenges, clone refusal,
  names, the suggestion after a code, autofill). The library: Rust's counterpart of `go-webauthn`
  is `webauthn-rs` (Kanidm's, MPL-2.0, maintained and reviewed), which uses OpenSSL; built with
  OpenSSL vendored (compiled from source by Cargo), nothing is installed on any system. Proposed
  instead of the own verifier of 10:45: the part of S3 where a mistake costs most is better in a
  library that others review, as intergate does (point 1 below).
- **S3-D7, HTTPS** (question 2): the service serves plain HTTP and no certificate; HTTPS is the
  proxy's (Caddy, nginx) in front, at the instance's address. With a proxy, the service trusts
  `X-Forwarded-Proto` and `X-Forwarded-For` only from addresses the owner lists (the proxy's;
  loopback by default), so codes' IP limits and secure cookies work behind it. `docs/usage.md`
  gets a Caddy and an nginx example. Reaching it over plain HTTP on the home network stays
  possible, off by default, with the warning.

### Question 5, with examples

The two switches give four ways to run an instance. What changes between them is who gets in and
whether a role protects anything:

| | One person | Several people |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Open** (no sign-in) | **A.** You, on your own computer: `http://localhost:4646`, no sign-in, you can do everything. This is jackbird today (S1, S2). | **B.** The living room computer: opening jackbird asks *"Who's listening? Ana · Luis · Kids"*. No codes, no passkeys: picking a name is enough. "Kids" is a listener, so the interface offers them no tag editing or renaming, and the log says that Luis renamed an album. But anyone can pick "Ana" (the owner): roles avoid mistakes, they do not protect. Open means the machine only, so B is people taking turns at one computer. |
| **Sign-in** | **C.** Only you, from anywhere: the service at home behind Caddy at `https://music.example.com`; from your phone or work you sign in with your email's code or your passkey. Nobody else can be invited. | **D.** The family instance: Ana (owner) invites Luis as an editor and the kids as listeners, each with their own email; each signs in with their code or passkey; the service refuses a listener's save even if they try it by hand. |

What B gives in S3 is little: per-person data (favourites, playlists, history, their own
metadata) is F3's, so in S3 a profile only decides which keys the interface shows and the name in
the log. It is worth it when a computer is shared and some people (children) should not edit by
mistake; with F3 each profile also gets its own playlists and favourites, which is when B makes
most sense.

### Left to the owner

1. **The passkeys' library**: `webauthn-rs` with vendored OpenSSL, as intergate uses a library
   (recommended), or jackbird's own verifier on RustCrypto (10:45's proposal: pure Rust, but
   security code nobody else reviews)?
2. **B (open, several people)**: in S3, small (profiles to pick, roles as defaults, the name in
   the log), or with F3, when profiles carry each person's own data (recommended now that F3's
   part is clear: in S3 it is little more than hiding keys)?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 11:46 — S3: how an instance goes from one person to several, and to sign-in (the owner's answer)

**Summary**: The owner answered 11:20's point 2 by describing the life of an instance. It always
starts with one person and no sign-in; turning sign-in on makes every later person sign up by
email and code; people added before sign-in (profiles, B of 11:20) are disabled when sign-in is
turned on, until an admin or owner gives each an email and they confirm it with a code. So B is
in S3, together with its way to D. S3's decisions are amended below; the passkeys' library (11:20,
point 1) is still open.

User message (translated): *"The installation will always start with one user and no sign-in.
If sign-in is added, the following users will require sign-up by email + OTP. If other users are
added, adding sign-in will disable the users without sign-in until they are validated with
email-OTP. The email will be added by an admin/owner."*

### The life of an instance (amends S3-D2, S3-D3 and S3-D5)

```
A (one person, open) ──add people──> B (several, open: profiles)
        │                                   │
   sign-in on                           sign-in on
        ▼                                   ▼
C (one person, sign-in) ──invite──> D (several, sign-in)
```

- **A, always the start**: a new installation has one person, the owner, with no email and no
  sign-in. That is S1 and S2 as they are.
- **A → B, adding people without sign-in**: the owner adds profiles, each a name and a role, with
  no email. The page asks *"Who's listening?"*; roles decide what the interface offers and the
  service accepts, and the log names who did what, but anyone at the machine can pick any profile
  (11:20's example). An open instance stays on the machine only.
- **A → C, turning sign-in on**: the first person gives their email and confirms it with a code
  (S3-D3: from the interface or from the console); they are the owner. From then on, every new
  person is invited by an owner or admin with an email and a role, and signs up with a code sent
  there (S3-D5).
- **B → D, turning sign-in on with profiles**: the person who turns it on confirms their email
  with a code and is an owner (their profile must be one). Every other profile is **disabled**:
  kept, with its role, its name in the log and (from F3) its own data, but it cannot be used.
  An owner or admin gives a disabled profile an email; an invitation is sent there, and once its
  code is typed the profile is enabled with everything it had. A profile with no email stays
  disabled; an owner or admin can remove it.
- **Turning sign-in off again** (proposed, point 2 below): an owner's, from the machine; people
  keep their emails and passkeys, become profiles again, and sessions end; the instance is back on
  the machine only.

### Left to the owner

1. **The passkeys' library** (11:20, point 1): `webauthn-rs` with vendored OpenSSL, as intergate
   uses a library (recommended), or jackbird's own verifier on RustCrypto?
2. **Turning sign-in off again** (D → B, C → A): as proposed above, or not offered once sign-in
   is on (only by the console, `jackbird-service signin off`)?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 11:51 — S3: turning sign-in on changes no role; only an owner turns it on (the owner's answer)

**Summary**: The owner corrected 11:46. Turning sign-in on changes nobody's role; the first person
of an installation is the owner, and can add admins and members; only an owner can turn sign-in
on. 11:46 is amended below. Whether "members" replaces 21:32's editors and listeners is asked.

User message (translated): *"Turning sign-in on must not change the role. The initial user is the
owner, and can add admins and members. Only an owner can turn sign-in on."*

### Amends 11:46

- **A**: the first person of every installation **is** the owner, from the start (not made one by
  turning sign-in on). They add admins and members (with sign-in off, as profiles; with it on, by
  invitation).
- **Turning sign-in on** (A → C, B → D) is an owner's only: from the interface, the profile in use
  must be an owner's; from the console (`jackbird-service setup`), access to the machine as the
  service's person stands for the owner. The owner confirms their email with a code; **no role
  changes**: the owner stays the owner, and every other profile keeps its role, disabled until an
  owner or admin gives it an email and its code is typed (11:46).
- 11:46's lines saying that the person turning sign-in on "is an owner" or "becomes the owner" are
  superseded by this.

### Left to the owner

1. **Roles**: "admins and members" — are there three roles now (owner, admin, member), a member
   doing what 21:32's editor did, or what its listener did? Or does "members" stand for 21:32's
   editors and listeners, and the four roles stay?
2. **More owners**: can an owner make other owners (21:32: "make and unmake admins and owners;
   there is always at least one owner"), or is the initial owner the only one?
3. **The passkeys' library** (11:20, point 1): `webauthn-rs` with vendored OpenSSL (recommended), or
   jackbird's own verifier?
4. **Turning sign-in off again** (11:46, point 2): an owner's, from the machine, people back to

## Extension — 2026-11-09 11:57 — S3: the owner's last answers; the plan as it stands

**Summary**: The owner answered the open points. The roles are owners, admins, **editors** and
listeners (21:32's names; 11:54's "member" is withdrawn). An owner promotes and demotes other
owners. Passkeys with `webauthn-rs`. Only an owner turns sign-in on or off. Every question of
S3 is answered; the plan as it stands is gathered below, for the owner's go.

User message (translated): *"1. Owners, admins, editors, listeners. 2. An owner can promote and
demote other owners. 3. Ok to webauthn-rs. 4. Only an owner can turn sign-in on/off."*

### S3, as it stands (10:45 amended at 11:20, 11:46, 11:51, 11:54 and here)

- **Modes** (11:46, 11:51): every installation starts in A, one person, the owner, no sign-in. The
  owner adds admins, editors and listeners: profiles with no email while sign-in is off (B), on the
  machine only. Only an owner turns sign-in on (A → C, B → D), confirming their email with a code,
  from the interface or the console (`jackbird-service setup`, `setup-url`); no role changes;
  the other profiles are disabled until an owner or admin gives each an email and its code is
  typed. Only an owner turns sign-in off again, from the interface or the console: people keep
  their emails and passkeys and are profiles again, sessions end, and the instance answers on the
  machine only again (the interface says so first, since an owner who turns it off from elsewhere
  loses the connection).
- **Roles** (21:32, S3-D9, 11:54 with editors): listeners read and play; editors also change the
  folders (tags, covers, writes, renames, folders, deletes, playlists); admins also add and invite
  people with roles up to editor, remove listeners and editors, give disabled profiles their email,
  read the log, set SMTP; owners also open and close folders, browse the machine's folders, turn
  sign-in on and off, set the address and the network, and promote and demote admins and owners,
  with at least one owner always left. The service enforces every route; every change to the
  folders is logged with who made it.
- **Sign-up** by invitation only (11:20).
- **Codes** (11:20, intergate's rules): six digits, 10 minutes, bound to the browser that asked,
  hashed, in memory, 5 tries, 5 per address in 15 minutes, 20 per IP in an hour; the same answer
  for unknown addresses. Every code goes by email; none is printed.
- **Passkeys** (11:20, intergate's choices): `webauthn-rs` with OpenSSL vendored; discoverable,
  user verification required, the instance's exact origin, a signed ceremony cookie (5 minutes),
  single-use challenges, a sign counter going back refused, named passkeys, the suggestion after a
  code, autofill; offered in a secure context only. Not in the desktop app's window in S3.
- **HTTPS** (11:20): a proxy in front (Caddy, nginx); `X-Forwarded-*` trusted from listed
  addresses only (loopback by default); the home network over plain HTTP possible, off by default,
  with a warning; Caddy and nginx examples in `docs/usage.md`.
- **Storage, sessions, interface, tests, steps**: S3-D1, S3-D8, S3-D10 and 10:45's tests and steps,
  unchanged; profiles (B) and the disabled profiles' email added to step 5.

## Extension — 2026-11-09 12:42 — S3, sign-in and people: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: S3 as approved at 11:57, in five commits on `ccr-2e54f236-r3l7nj`: the service's
people, roles, sessions, codes, SMTP and sign-in (`b1b1dbc`); passkeys (`d048664`); the
interface (`310f33f`); the docs and CI (`dcf4d70`). Its "done when" is met here: with sign-in on,
an editor and a listener each do what their role allows and nothing more, in Chromium against the
built service. Departures from the plan, each found while doing it, are listed below.

### What there is

- **`crates/service`**: `people.rs` (`jackbird.db`: people, sessions kept as hashes, passkeys, the
  log; the first person, the owner, made with the database; the file the person's alone),
  `auth.rs` (signed tokens, codes and their limits, single-use ceremonies), `access.rs` (who
  is asking: a cookie, a bearer, the console's key, a media token, or the one person of an open
  instance; `require` on every route by role), `people_api.rs` (state, profiles, codes, sessions,
  people, the log, SMTP, settings, setup, sign-in off), `passkeys.rs` (`webauthn-rs`), `mail.rs`
  (`lettre`, texts in English and Spanish). The guard: open, the machine only (a peer elsewhere,
  or a forwarded request, refused); with sign-in on, any `Host`, `X-Forwarded-*` from the listed
  proxies, `__Host-` secure cookies over HTTPS. `main.rs`: the home network listened on while
  wanted; `jackbird-service setup` and `signin off`.
- **The web**: `lib/account.ts` and `views/signin-dialog.html` (profiles, code, passkey);
  Preferences' Account (name, passkeys, sessions, sign out or switch profile);
  `lib/people.ts` and `views/people-dialog.html` (people and roles, email, the address and the
  network, sign-in on and off, the log); `can(role)` in the menus, the selection's tabs and the
  folder key; helpers `roles.ts`, `passkeys.ts`, `agent.ts` with their tests.
- **Checks**: 25 Rust tests through the router (every route against every role, codes and limits,
  the transitions A → B → D and back, admins' and owners' rules, the last owner, the proxy, the
  console, media tokens; passkeys with a P-256 software authenticator of the tests' own: added,
  signed in without an email, a replay, an old assertion, another origin, another RP ID, a count
  going back, a passkey never added, a disabled person). `scripts/signin-check.cjs` with
  `scripts/smtp-sink.mjs` and Chromium's virtual authenticator, in `service.yml` on the three
  systems.

### Departures from the plan

1. **Invitations without a link of their own**: an invitation is an email saying that the address
   was given a place, and where to sign in; the first code sent to it is the sign-up. A token in a
   link would prove nothing the code does not.
2. **No `setup-url`**: an open instance answers on its machine only, so a link for a browser
   elsewhere could not be opened. Without a screen: `jackbird-service setup` in the console, or the
   interface through `ssh -L 4646:localhost:4646`.
3. **The home network is each of the machine's addresses**, looked at again every 30 s, not
   `0.0.0.0`: on Linux the loopback's port is in the way of the wildcard.
4. **A listener keeps no `.meta/` snapshot** (the plan said "in the browser"): the folder is read
   again each time. Playlists are offered to editors only.
5. **Found by the check**: the one person of an open instance had no session (they were
   everyone), so adding a second made them pick a profile; they now get one with that answer.
   And non-owners, who may not open folders, saw none: everyone now sees the folders the service
   has open.
6. **The log leaves out `.meta/`'s writes**: the interface's cache, not a change to the catalogue.
7. **Windows CI**: OpenSSL built from source adds about ten minutes to each Windows build.

### Verified here (Linux)

`cargo test`, clippy and rustfmt for the service; `npm test` (321 tests, 23 helpers at the
floor); `scripts/service-check.cjs` (S1's check, unchanged: open, one person); the console's
`setup` against a real SMTP exchange with the sink, and the home network listened on and dropped
as sign-in was turned on and off; `scripts/signin-check.cjs` end to end:

```
owner: the folder is open
owner: email set up
owner: an editor and a listener added, as profiles
owner: sign-in is on, still in; invitations to editor@example.com, kids@example.com
editor: signed in with the code, and sees the owner's folder
editor: a passkey added, signed out, and back in with it
editor: a title saved
listener: editing offered 0 times; the service answers 403 and 403
owner: the log shows Luis's save
after a restart: the editor is still in, with the folder
```

### Left to the owner, by hand

A real SMTP server; HTTPS through Caddy or nginx at the instance's address, with a passkey on a
phone and on a computer; the home network from another device over plain HTTP; the desktop app as
the interface of a service with sign-in on (it signs in by code and keeps a token).

## Extension — 2026-11-09 13:20 — S4, the online route: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for S4 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-08/service.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Ok, cuando quieras sigue con S4"*): the service at home keeps an outbound SSH connection to a
server on the internet and has it forward a port of that server back to itself (a reverse
tunnel, as intergate does), so a proxy there serves the instance's address over HTTPS with
nothing opened at home. Nine decisions are proposed; five questions are left to the owner.
Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- S3: with sign-in on, the service answers any `Host`, believes `X-Forwarded-*` from the listed
  proxies (loopback by default), sets `__Host-` secure cookies over HTTPS, and binds passkeys to
  the instance's address. An open instance refuses forwarded requests.
- 14:57 (2026-11-08): the route is an outbound SSH connection from the local server to an online
  one, as intergate does; jackbird stays independent of intergate (21:40, 2026-11-04).
- Nothing in jackbird speaks SSH yet.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S4-D1 | **The service keeps the tunnel itself**, with an SSH client in the binary: `russh` (Apache-2.0, pure Rust, with `ring`, already in the tree through `lettre`). It asks the server to listen on a port (`tcpip-forward`) and, for each connection the server forwards, opens one to its own `127.0.0.1:<port>` and copies bytes both ways. Requests through the route therefore reach the service from the machine itself, so S3's rules apply as they are: `X-Forwarded-*` (set by the server's proxy) believed, cookies secure. | no `ssh` to install (Windows), the service knows and shows the route's state, and reconnects it itself |
| S4-D2 | **Only with sign-in on, and an address set**: the route does not start otherwise, and stops when sign-in is turned off. | an open instance is anyone's who reaches it (21:35) |
| S4-D3 | **jackbird's own key**: an Ed25519 key made the first time, kept beside the configuration (the person's alone); the interface shows its public line to paste into the server's `authorized_keys`, with the restriction that lets it do nothing but this forward (`restrict,port-forwarding,permitlisten="localhost:4646"`). | the key opens nothing else on the server |
| S4-D4 | **The server is pinned**: the first connection shows the server's key fingerprint and waits for the owner to confirm it; it is kept, and a different one later stops the route and says so. | nobody in the middle can pose as the server |
| S4-D5 | **Where the server listens**: a bind address and a port the owner sets, `localhost` and 4646 by default, so only the server's own proxy reaches it (no `GatewayPorts`). The bind address is free text, so a server that names routes by it works too (intergate's `ssh -R jackbird:80:…`). | the proxy is the only way in, and it brings HTTPS |
| S4-D6 | **Kept up**: keepalives every 30 s; reconnection with a growing wait (1 s to 1 min) after a drop, a network change or a restart of the server; its state (connected since, the last error) in the interface and the log. | the route survives a home connection's hiccups |
| S4-D7 | **The server's side is documented, not shipped**: `docs/usage.md` gets the account and its `authorized_keys` line, `sshd_config` (`AllowTcpForwarding remote`), and Caddy (`music.example.com { reverse_proxy localhost:4646 }`) or nginx; intergate as one more such server. | any SSH server with a proxy works; jackbird is not tied to one |
| S4-D8 | **In the interface**: Sign-in gains "Online route" (owners): the server (host, SSH port, user), the bind address and port, the public key with Copy, the fingerprint to confirm, a switch, and its state. The console: `jackbird-service route on|off|status`. Texts in English and Spanish. | the owner's "set up from the interface" |
| S4-D9 | **Checks**: Rust tests against an SSH server made with `russh`'s own server side, in process (the forward asked for, a forwarded connection reaching `/api/info`, a dropped connection retried, a changed server key refused, the route refused with sign-in off); on Linux in CI, a real OpenSSH `sshd` on the runner and the unattended check through it: Chromium reaches the forwarded port, signs in and plays. | the step's "done when" with a real server, without a server of our own on the internet |

### Not in S4

A relay mode of jackbird's binary for the server (question 2), the server's TLS (its proxy's),
several routes at once, the desktop app keeping a route of its own (only the service does).

### Steps (estimates)

1. The tunnel: `russh`, the key, the pinning, the bridge to the service's port, reconnection;
   tests with an in-process SSH server (1.5 d).
2. Its settings, routes and console commands; the guard's rules (sign-in on, an address) (0.5 d).
3. The interface's "Online route" (1 d).
4. CI's check through a real `sshd` on Linux; the docs (Caddy, nginx, sshd); the walkthrough (1 d).

### Done when

S4's row: the service is reached from outside home through an online server, with nothing opened
at home.

### Questions for the owner

1. **The SSH client**: inside the binary (`russh`, recommended: nothing to install on Windows,
   the service owns reconnection and state), or the system's `ssh` run by the service?
2. **The server's side**: documented, any SSH server plus a proxy (recommended for S4), or also a
   relay mode of jackbird's binary for the server, which would set up the proxy and certificates
   itself (a step of its own)?
3. **Where the server listens**: on its own `localhost`, only its proxy reaching it (recommended),
   or on every address (`GatewayPorts`), reachable directly without HTTPS?
4. **The server's key**: confirmed by the owner on the first connection (recommended), or pasted
   in beforehand?
5. **Its key**: one Ed25519 key that jackbird makes (recommended), or also one of the owner's own
   keys?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 13:46 — F1, the family library (the store): implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for F1 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/family-instance.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Vale, sigue con F1"*), as the service (S1 to S3) now stands: one shared library, kept by the
service in a store of its own, where each song is stored once, immutable, named by the SHA-256 of
its audio, and its tags live in layers (the file's own, and the family's edits) that are written
into the file only as it leaves (playback, downloads). F0 is folded in: its passkey and route
questions were answered by S3 and by intergate (S4 postponed); its store layout is F1's first
step. Ten decisions are proposed; five questions are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented
until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- The service (S1) serves **folders as they are**: an owner opens one, everyone sees it (S3), and
  an editor's save rewrites the file in place (`crates/core::save`, a splice of its tags).
- `crates/tags` reads a file's tags from its head (`read_head`), finds its audio region
  (`layout`), and writes new tags as a **splice**: the bytes `0..tags_end` replaced, the rest of
  the file (its audio, and in FLAC the `STREAMINFO` kept in the head) untouched. That is what
  lets a stored file stay as it was imported and still leave with the family's tags.
- Checksums today are SHA-1 (E-D5: SHA-256 waits for `.meta` v2); 21:32 (2026-11-04) decided
  SHA-256 of the audio region as the store's names.
- People, roles and the log are S3's (`jackbird.db`).

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F1-D1 | **In the service, a crate of its own**: `crates/store` (no HTTP): the blobs, the catalogue in `jackbird.db`, the layers, the bytes out; `crates/service` puts it behind HTTP. No `crates/server`: 14:14 (2026-11-08) made the service F1's backend. | one binary, one database, the same people and roles |
| F1-D2 | **Where it lives**: a data folder the owner sets (by default `~/.local/share/jackbird/library`, `~/Library/Application Support/jackbird/library`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\jackbird\library`): `blobs/ab/cd/<sha256>.<mp3|flac>`, `covers/<sha256>`, and `files/` for the interface's small files (its `.meta/` snapshot, playlists). | a store, not a folder to browse (21:32); easy to back up (F4) |
| F1-D3 | **A blob is the imported file, whole and never changed**, named by the SHA-256 of its audio region (question 1). The same audio imported again is not stored twice. Its own tags are the **file's layer**, read once and kept in the catalogue. | FLAC needs its `STREAMINFO` to play; the file as it came is kept, nothing is lost |
| F1-D4 | **Two layers per song** (21:32): the file's (as imported) and the family's (what editors set, field by field, covers included, as blobs in `covers/`). What everyone sees is the family's over the file's. An editor's save writes the family's layer only; no file is rewritten. Every change is logged (S3). Each person's own layer is F3's. | edits are cheap, reversible and never risk a file |
| F1-D5 | **Out with the family's tags**: playback and downloads get `write_head` of the file's head with the family's layer, then the blob from `tags_end` on, with byte ranges over the whole (the head is small and kept in memory, cached per song and layer version). A song nobody edited goes out as it is. | the "tags written on the fly" of 21:32; a download is a normal file any player reads |
| F1-D6 | **The library is a root to the interface**: a new source kind, `library`, with the operations the views already use (walk, inspect, save, media, small files, rename, delete), so the views barely change. Its paths are the songs' places in the library (by default the paths they were imported with, under the import's name; question 4); renaming moves a song in the catalogue, not on disk; deleting takes it out of the catalogue (its blob stays until F4's trash and scrub). Everyone with a role sees it; the folders of S1 stay, for owners (question 2). | the web, the desktop app and later a phone use one source; the views are not rewritten |
| F1-D7 | **Importing** (21:32: editors add music): (a) **from a folder of the machine**, owners, through S1's folder dialog: walked and copied, streamed, never moved or changed; (b) **from the browser**, editors and up: files picked or dropped, uploaded one at a time, streamed, with progress (question 3). Each import ends with a report: added, already there, already there with other tags, unreadable. | the family adds music from their own devices, not only the owner from the machine |
| F1-D8 | **The same audio with other tags** (question 5): the song is not stored again; the import's tags are kept beside it as a suggestion for editors, shown in the song's Info, until F3 makes them the importer's own layer. | nothing anyone brought is lost; F3 decides how it is used |
| F1-D9 | **SHA-256, streamed**: `crates/core` gains SHA-256 of a file's audio region, read in chunks (the audio never held whole); the browser's SHA-1 checksums stay as they are for folders. | 21:32's names; files of any size |
| F1-D10 | **Checks**: `cargo test` on `crates/store` (import, the same audio twice, layers, what goes out read back by `crates/tags` equal to what everyone sees, ranges across the head and the blob, a rename, a removal) and on the service's routes against the roles. An unattended check in `service.yml` on the three systems: the owner imports the fixtures' folder, an editor uploads a file and retitles a song, a listener sees the new title, plays it, and a download carries it; the files on disk unchanged. | the step's "done when" |

### Not in F1

Each person's own data and layer (F3); concurrent edits and versions, backups, the trash and the
scrub, the plain-folder export (F4); the Docker image (F5); OpenSubsonic (the owner's study);
moving S1's folders into the store in place (an import copies).

### Steps (estimates)

1. `crates/store`: the blobs, the catalogue, the layers, import from a folder, the bytes out with
   ranges; SHA-256 in `crates/core`; tests (3.5 d).
2. The service's routes for the library (the root's operations, import from the machine, upload,
   downloads, the report) behind S3's roles; tests (2 d).
3. The interface: the library as a source, import (machine and upload) with progress and its
   report, Info's suggestion and download; texts in both languages (2.5 d).
4. The unattended check, the docs, the walkthrough, the roadmap (1.5 d).

### Done when

F1's row: a family library imports, plays in the web and the app, and a tag edit by one person
shows for another.

### Questions for the owner

1. **What a blob is**: the imported file whole, its own tags inside it as the file's layer
   (recommended: FLAC needs its head to play, and nothing is lost), or the audio region alone,
   every byte of tags kept in the catalogue and the head rebuilt on the way out?
2. **Folders and the library**: both, folders for owners as today and the library for everyone
   (recommended), or the library only, folders reached only by importing them?
3. **Uploads from the browser in F1**: yes, editors add music from their own devices
   (recommended), or only owners importing from the machine for now?
4. **Where songs sit in the library**: the paths they were imported with, under the import's
   name, rearranged by renaming as today (recommended), or arranged by their tags
   (`Artist/Album/NN - Title`) on import?
5. **The same audio with other tags**: kept as a suggestion beside the song until F3
   (recommended), or the second import's tags ignored, only reported?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 14:35 — F1, the family library: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: F1 as approved (13:46, its five recommendations), in four commits on
`ccr-2e54f236-r3l7nj`: `crates/store` and SHA-256 in `crates/core` (`101fa43`); the library
over HTTP (`fb75bc2`); the interface and its unattended check (`98113b4`); the docs, this
walkthrough and the roadmap. Its "done when" is met here: a family library imports, plays in the
web, and a tag edit by one person shows for another. Departures and findings below.

### What there is

- **`crates/store`**: blobs (the imported file whole, named by the SHA-256 of its audio), the
  catalogue (`catalogue.db` in the library's folder: songs, their file's tags, the family's
  layer and its version, suggestions), covers by their SHA-256, small files in `files/`. Import
  from a folder (copied, the copy hashed again before it is renamed in) or of an upload (moved in);
  saves to the family's layer, tried first on the song's head; `Outgoing`: the head with the
  layer written in, then the blob, with ranges; the library as a root (walk, list, stat, read and
  write small files, rename and delete in the catalogue).
- **`crates/core`**: `examine` (the SHA-1s and the audio's SHA-256 in one pass) and `head_of`.
- **The service**: `/api/library/*` (listeners: read, inspect, play, download, suggestions, the
  import's state; editors: save, rename, delete, small files, upload; owners: import a folder of
  the machine, in the background), songs streamed out with ranges; the library's folder in
  `service.json` (`library`), the person's data folder by default; every change logged.
- **The web**: the `library` source, first among the service's roots; **Add music** (upload songs
  or a folder; owners also import a folder of the machine) with progress and a report; Info's
  Download and the suggestions.
- **Checks**: 6 tests in `crates/store`, 2 more through the service's router (an owner's
  import, an editor's upload and save, a listener's ranges and download read back by the engine,
  each role's refusals, the log); `scripts/library-check.cjs` in `service.yml`.

### Findings and departures

1. **The fixtures share their audio**: of the 26, there are three audio regions (the MP3s, the
   MP3 with APEv2, the FLACs), so a library of them keeps three songs. That is the store doing what
   it should; its tests make fixtures distinct by adding bytes at their end.
2. **Removing a FLAC's cover can show its next picture** (the engine removes the picture shown and
   keeps the others): the cover the interface shows is read from what the song leaves with when
   the family's layer touches it, so what is shown is what is downloaded.
3. **A name that says another format** gets the content's extension on import.
4. **An empty library hid a folder**: with no active root kept, the first root with songs is shown.
5. **"Add music" broke the checks' `Music` button**: they now ask for a name starting with it.
6. Not in F1, as planned: each person's layer and data (F3), versions and the trash (F4), the
   plain-folder export, Docker (F5).

### Verified here (Linux)

`cargo test` (store, service, core), clippy and rustfmt; `npm test` (321 tests, the floors);
S1's and S3's checks; `scripts/library-check.cjs`:

```
sign-in on, an editor and a listener invited
owner imported Music: 2 added, 0 already there, 0 already there with other tags, 0 left out.
editor uploaded a song: 1 added, 0 already there, 0 already there with other tags, 0 left out.
editor retitled a song
listener played it ( 206 206 ) and downloaded 4595 bytes carrying the title: true
the files on the machine are as they were
```

### Left to the owner, by hand

A real library imported from a disk (hundreds of songs, the time it takes, the report), an upload
from a phone, the desktop app as the interface of a service with a library, a download opened in
another player.

## Extension — 2026-11-09 14:39 — F3, each person's own: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for F3 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/family-instance.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Ok, dale"*, after F1's walkthrough): in the family library, each person has favourites, a
history of what they played, playlists of their own, their settings, and a metadata layer of their
own over the family's, with a switch between the family's version and theirs; an import with other
tags becomes the importer's layer and stays a suggestion for editors. Eight decisions are proposed;
four questions are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- F1: songs in `crates/store` with two layers (file, family) and suggestions; the library is a
  root; its playlists are `.m3u` files in its `files/`, shared, editors' to change.
- S3: people (profiles with sign-in off, accounts with it on), roles; the web keeps the theme and
  the language in the browser (`src/lib/storage.ts`), the queue and the open roots in IndexedDB.
- There are no favourites and no history anywhere yet.
- 21:32 (2026-11-04): a listener has their own favourites, playlists, history, settings and
  version of the metadata; editors edit the shared catalogue.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F3-D1 | **Where it lives**: what is about songs (favourites, plays, personal playlists, personal layers) in the library's `catalogue.db`, by person id, so the library's folder stays the whole library; settings with the person in `jackbird.db`. Removing a person removes theirs. | one backup of the library holds everyone's own |
| F3-D2 | **Favourites**: a heart on a song (its row, the player), on or off; a **Favourites** view of the library. | the simplest "mine" |
| F3-D3 | **History**: a play counts once the song has played half its length or four minutes (as most players count); a **Recently played** view; "Clear my history" in Account (question 2). | what was listened to, per person |
| F3-D4 | **Personal playlists**: anyone, listeners included, makes playlists of library songs that are theirs (question 1); the shared ones (`.m3u` in the library) stay as they are, editors'. Both shown in the Playlists view, each marked. | 21:32's "their own playlists" without touching the shared ones |
| F3-D5 | **A layer of one's own**: saving tags asks *for everyone* (editors and up; the family's layer, as F1) or *only for me* (anyone, listeners included; their layer). What a person sees, plays and downloads is their layer over the family's over the file's; **Account** has the switch *Show: the family's version / mine*, and a song's Info shows where each field comes from with "Back to the family's" per field (question 3). | 21:32's own version, the family's untouched |
| F3-D6 | **Imports with other tags**: the same audio brought with other tags becomes the importer's own layer (they see their tags at once) and stays a suggestion for editors, who can apply it to the family's layer from Info (question 4). F1's suggestions already kept are turned into their importers' layers. | the roadmap's F3; nothing anyone brought is lost |
| F3-D7 | **Settings follow the person**: theme and language kept with the person on the service and applied wherever they sign in or pick their profile; the browser keeps its copy for the moment before. | the same jackbird on every device |
| F3-D8 | **Checks**: `crates/store` tests (three layers, the switch, favourites, plays counted once, personal playlists, an import becoming a layer); the service's routes against the roles (anyone writes only their own; a listener cannot write the family's); `library-check.cjs` extended: two people see the same song with different titles, the family's version untouched, a favourite and a play in each one's views. | the step's "done when" |

### Not in F3

Shared listening or recommendations; folders (S1 roots) keep no per-person data (only the library
does); concurrent edits and versions (F4).

### Steps (estimates)

1. `crates/store`: personal layers and the switch, favourites, plays, personal playlists, imports
   as layers; tests (1.5 d).
2. The service's routes, settings with the person; tests (1 d).
3. The interface: the heart, Favourites and Recently played, personal playlists, "for everyone /
   only for me", Info's sources per field and "apply to the family", Account's switch and history,
   settings synced; texts in both languages (2 d).
4. The check, the docs, the walkthrough (0.5 d).

### Done when

F3's row: two people see the same song with different titles, and the family's version is
untouched.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Personal playlists**: private to their person (recommended for F3), or also shareable with
   the others, read-only?
2. **History**: kept until the person clears it (recommended), or only the last N plays?
3. **Editors saving tags**: asked *for everyone / only for me* on each save, *for everyone* first
   (recommended), or a setting chosen once?
4. **Imports with other tags**: the importer's layer and a suggestion (recommended), or only a
   suggestion, as in F1?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 14:53 — F3, each person's own: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: F3 as approved (14:39, its four recommendations), in three commits on
`ccr-2e54f236-r3l7nj`: the store and the service (`83523c8`), the interface and its check
(`8c22d61`), the docs and this walkthrough. Its "done when" is met here: two people see the same
song with different titles, and the family's version is untouched. Departures below.

### What there is

- **`crates/store`** (`own.rs`): own layers, favourites, plays, own files, by person; a
  `Viewer` for every read and every way out; `Scope` for saves; F1's `inspect`, `save` and
  `outgoing` are the family's view of the general ones. An import with other tags becomes the
  importer's layer (only the fields that differ) and a suggestion; `apply_suggestion`.
- **The service**: every library route sees as the person does (their setting `mine`); anyone
  writes in their own folder and saves their own layer, the rest stays editors'; `/library/layers`,
  `/mine/forget`, `/favourite`, `/played`, `/history/clear`, `/suggestions/apply`
  (editors); `/me/settings` (theme, language, `mine`), kept with the person.
- **The web**: `lib/mine.ts`; the editor's *For everyone / Only for me* (listeners edit their
  own version in the library, *Saved for you only*); Info's own fields with *Back to the family's
  version* and suggestions with *Apply for everyone*; *Add to favourites* in a song's menu; a play
  counted at half the song or four minutes (`helpers/plays.ts`); Account's *Your version* switch
  and *Clear what I played*; theme and language followed.
- **Checks**: 3 more store tests, 1 more through the service's router (two titles, the switch,
  downloads as each sees them, favourites, plays, own playlists, the roles); `library-check.cjs`
  extended in Chromium.

### Departures from the plan

1. **Favourites and Recently played are playlists**, not views of their own: `My playlists` is a
   folder of the library that is each person's (their playlists, and those two made from what they
   did), so the playlists' code, dialogs and menus work on them unchanged. The heart is a menu
   item (*Add to favourites*), not a key on every row and on the player.
2. **Info's "back to the family's" is for the whole of one's version of a song**, not field by
   field; the fields that are one's own are listed.
3. **F1's suggestions kept before F3 stay suggestions**: they recorded the importer's name, not
   who they were, so they do not become anyone's layer; new imports do.
4. **Changing *Your version* reloads the page**: every song's checksum changes for that person.
5. **The names `Favourites` and `Recently played` are in English** whatever the language, as
   are `My playlists` and `Uploads`: they are paths. Showing them translated is left for later.

### Verified here (Linux)

`cargo test` (store 9, service 28), clippy, rustfmt; `npm test` (324 tests, 24 helpers at the
floor); S1's and S3's checks; `library-check.cjs`:

Two people on two devices with a real library: one's own titles, favourites and playlists; the

## Extension — 2026-11-09 15:18 — F4, safety: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for F4 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/family-instance.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Redacta el plan de F4"*): edits made against an old version are refused and shown; the
library's databases snapshotted every night and rotated; deleted songs kept in a trash for 30 days
before their blobs go; a weekly scrub that hashes every blob again; a copy away from home with
restic, documented and checked; and an export of the library to a plain folder. Eight decisions
are proposed; five questions are left to the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- F1 and F3: songs in `crates/store` (blobs named by the SHA-256 of their audio, never changed),
  their family's layer with a version and each person's own with theirs; the full checksum the
  interface keeps carries both. A save today writes over whatever is there. Deleting a song takes
  it out of the catalogue; its blob stays, with nothing to clean it up or to bring the song back.
- Small files (the library's `.meta/` and shared playlists) are written whole; two people editing
  one shared playlist at once lose one's change.
- The library's folder holds blobs, covers, small files and `catalogue.db`; people, sessions and
  settings are in `jackbird.db` beside the configuration.
- 21:32 (2026-11-04) proposed the backups (3-2-1: snapshots built in, restic to a second place, the
  plain-folder export) and roles: admins move music to the trash; owners do storage, backups and
  restores.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F4-D1 | **Saves carry the version they read**: the interface sends the full checksum it holds with each save; when it is no longer the song's (someone saved since), the save is refused (`409 stale`), nothing is written, and the dialog shows who changed it, with the song's tags as they are now, for the person to apply theirs again (question 1). One's own layer counts too, so two of one's devices cannot overwrite each other. | an edit is never lost without anyone knowing |
| F4-D2 | **Shared playlists too**: a write of a small file of the library carries the date and size it read; a changed one is refused and the playlist read again, the person's change offered again on top of it. One's own playlists (F3) are theirs alone and keep writing as they do. | the family's playlists are where two people would edit at once |
| F4-D3 | **The trash**: deleting a song (editors and up) moves it to the trash, with who and when; **Trash** in People and sign-in lists it, and admins and owners restore a song or empty the trash; after 30 days a song is gone from the catalogue and its blob is deleted when no song uses it any more (question 2). Its layers, favourites and plays go with it only then. | a mistake can be undone; blobs never pile up |
| F4-D4 | **Nightly snapshots**: at 03:00 the service copies `catalogue.db` and `jackbird.db` with SQLite's `VACUUM INTO` into the library's `backups/`, kept 7 daily, 4 weekly, 12 monthly (question 3); blobs and covers need none, they never change. An owner can take one by hand. | a damaged or mistaken database comes back as it was the night before |
| F4-D5 | **The weekly scrub**: once a week, slowly (one blob at a time, paused while songs are being imported), every blob's audio is hashed again and compared with its name; one that does not match (a failing disk) is reported in Backups and the log, with the songs that use it, to be restored from a copy. | a bad disk is found before the copy away from home is overwritten with it |
| F4-D6 | **Away from home, with restic**: the library's folder (blobs, covers, small files, `backups/`) is what to copy; `docs/usage.md` gets the steps with restic to a USB disk or a storage service (B2, a Hetzner Storage Box), encrypted before it leaves, and how to restore it: stop the service, `restic restore`, put the newest snapshot in place, start it. The service does not run restic itself (question 5). | the owner's own tool and place; jackbird only makes the folder easy to copy |
| F4-D7 | **The plain-folder export**: an owner exports the library to a folder of the machine as `Artist/Album/NN - Title.ext`, each song with the family's tags written in (question 4), in the background, with progress and a report, like an import; any player reads it, and it is a copy with no lock-in. | 21:32's "no lock-in" |
| F4-D8 | **Checks**: `crates/store` tests (a stale save refused, own and family; the trash restored and expired, its blob kept while another song uses it; a snapshot taken and rotated; the scrub finding a changed blob; the export's tree and tags); the service's routes against the roles; in CI on Linux, `library-check.cjs` extended: two browsers edit one song and the second is refused and shown, a song deleted and restored, and the library backed up with restic, wiped, restored and seen again. | the step's "done when" |

### Not in F4

Merging two edits field by field; the service running restic or keeping credentials for a storage
service; versions of a song's layers to go back to (the snapshots are the way back); the folders
of S1 (only the library).

### Steps (estimates)

1. Versions on saves and on shared small files; the trash and its expiry; tests (1.5 d).
2. Snapshots and their rotation, the scrub, the export; tests (1.5 d).
3. The service's routes and timers; the interface: the stale save shown, Trash, Backups (the last
   snapshot, the scrub's result, Take a snapshot, Export); texts in both languages (1.5 d).
4. The restic steps in the docs; the check with restic in CI; the walkthrough (0.5 d).

### Done when

F4's row: an edit made against an old version is refused, and a restore from the off-site copy
brings back the library.

### Questions for the owner

1. **A stale save**: refused, the newer tags shown and the person's own applied again by hand
   (recommended), or merged by the service when the two changed different fields?
2. **The trash**: editors and up delete into it, admins and owners restore and empty it, 30 days
   before it is emptied by itself (recommended), or another length, or a setting?
3. **Snapshots**: every night at 03:00, 7 daily, 4 weekly, 12 monthly (recommended), or a
   setting?
4. **The export**: with the family's version (recommended), or a person's own version to choose?
5. **restic**: documented, the owner runs it (recommended for F4), or the service running it on a
   schedule with the repository's password kept by it (a step of its own)?

## Extension — 2026-11-09 17:02 — F4, safety: walkthrough (Linux verified here; macOS and Windows in CI)

**Summary**: F4 as approved (15:18, its five recommendations), in three commits on
`ccr-2e54f236-r3l7nj`: the store (`94285e6`), the service and its timers (`6220a56`), the
interface and the check with restic (`8e2d32b`), and the docs with this walkthrough. Its "done
when" is met here: an edit made against an old version is refused, and a restore from the copy
made with restic brings back the library. Departures below.

### What there is

- **`crates/store`** (`safety.rs`): `save_checked` (the full checksum read, as the saver sees
  the song; `Stale` with the song now and who changed it), `write_checked` (size and date read),
  the trash (`delete_by`, `trash`, `restore`, `empty_trash`, `expire_trash`; trashed songs
  left out of every list, their places kept, an import of their audio bringing them back),
  `snapshot`/`backups`/`rotate_backups` with `keep_backups` (7 daily, 4 weekly, 12 monthly),
  `scrub`, `export`.
- **The service**: `409 stale` on saves and small files; deletes to the trash; `/library/trash`,
  `/restore`, `/empty` (admins); `/library/backups`, `/snapshot`, `/scrub`, `/export`
  (owners); snapshots of `jackbird.db` beside the catalogue's; the timers.
- **The web**: the expected checksum on library saves; a stale save shows the song's tags as they
  are now in the form; shared playlists written with what was read; Trash and Backups in People
  and sign-in.
- **Checks**: 7 more store tests, 1 more through the router; `library-check.cjs` with a stale save
  between two browsers, the trash, a snapshot, and restic (backed up, wiped, restored, seen again);
  restic installed on the Linux runner.

### Departures from the plan

1. **The night's snapshot is after 03:00 UTC**, not local time: the service has no time zone of its
   own to go by without a dependency for it; said in the docs.
2. **A refused playlist write is not offered again on top of the newer one**: the change is refused
   and said so; the playlist is read again when the person opens it.
3. **Who changed a song is shown in the service's answer**, but the editor's message is the
   generic "changed since you opened it" (the interface's error carries a code and a message).
4. **The restic check runs where restic is installed** (the Linux runner, and here); on macOS and
   Windows its part is skipped.

### Verified here (Linux)

`cargo test` (store 16, service 29), clippy, rustfmt; `npm test`; S1's and S3's checks;
`library-check.cjs`:

```
owner's save on an old version refused; the form shows: Second edit by Luis
a song deleted into the trash and restored
the library backed up with restic, wiped, restored and seen again
```

### Left to the owner, by hand

restic to a real place away from home (a USB disk kept elsewhere, B2, a Storage Box) and a restore
from it; the night's snapshot after a real night; an export opened in another player.

## Extension — 2026-11-09 17:10 — F5, shipping: implementation plan

**Summary**: The plan for F5 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/family-instance.md`, at the owner's request
(*"Ok, adelante con F5"*): the service as a Docker image and as plain binaries for Linux (x64 and
arm64, for a NAS or a Raspberry Pi) and macOS, built in GitHub Actions and attached to each
release; a compose file with the service and restic; and the docs to install it on a clean
machine and reach it from outside home. Seven decisions are proposed; five questions are left to
the owner. Nothing is implemented until it is approved.

### Where it starts

- `service.yml` builds `jackbird-service` with the web embedded on Linux (x64, glibc), macOS
  (arm64) and Windows (x64); `release.yml` attaches those binaries to each release.
- The service listens on the loopback address; open (sign-in off), it refuses any peer that is
  not the machine itself. Inside a container, a request through a published port comes from the
  container network's gateway, not from the loopback: an open instance in Docker would refuse
  everyone.
- Its configuration, people and console key live in the configuration folder; the library (F1)
  in the data folder; both follow `JACKBIRD_SERVICE_CONFIG`, `XDG_DATA_HOME` and `service.json`'s
  `library`. `jackbird-service setup` runs on the same machine as the service.
- F4 left restic to the owner, documented.

### Decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F5-D1 | **Linux binaries, static**: `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` and `aarch64-unknown-linux-musl`, built natively on GitHub's x64 and arm64 Ubuntu runners (`musl-tools`; SQLite and OpenSSL are already built from source), so one file runs on any Linux, a NAS's or a Raspberry Pi's included (question 2). The glibc build stays for the desktop app's sidecar. | no "GLIBC_2.xx not found" on an older NAS |
| F5-D2 | **macOS**: arm64 and x64, each on its own runner (question 5). Windows as today. | Intel Macs still serve music at home |
| F5-D3 | **The Docker image**: Alpine with `ca-certificates` (SMTP over TLS) and the static binary of its architecture, built per architecture on native runners and joined into one multi-arch image; `/config` (configuration, people, the console's key) and `/library` (the family library) as volumes; port 4646; a health check on `/api/info`. Published to GitHub's registry, `ghcr.io/jgermade/jackbird`, on each release (`:<version>` and `:latest`; question 3). | small, the same binary as the release's, one name for every machine |
| F5-D4 | **Open in a container** (question 1): `JACKBIRD_LISTEN=0.0.0.0` makes it listen on every address, and `JACKBIRD_MACHINE=<networks>` names the networks that count as "the machine" for an open instance (the container's bridge, so the host reaches it); the compose file publishes the port on the host's loopback only (`127.0.0.1:4646:4646`), so an open instance is still the host's alone. With sign-in on, the home network and a proxy work as they do outside a container. | open stays "this machine only", whether or not it runs in Docker |
| F5-D5 | **`compose.yaml`** in `deploy/`: jackbird (volumes, the loopback port, a read-only `/music` to import from), and restic beside it, the official `restic/restic` image looping `restic backup /library` and `restic forget` once a day, its repository and password from an `.env` file the owner writes (question 4); Caddy in front as a commented, optional service. The console in a container: `docker compose exec jackbird jackbird-service setup`. | 21:32's "a compose file with the server and restic" |
| F5-D6 | **The docs**: `docs/install.md`, three ways (Docker Compose; a binary with `jackbird-service install --with-system` on Linux and macOS; the desktop app, S2), each from a clean machine to the first owner by email and a code; reaching it from outside home (a proxy with HTTPS on the home network, or intergate's tunnel: S4 postponed); updating; backups (F4). README links to it. | an instance installs from the docs alone |
| F5-D7 | **Checks**: on Linux in CI, the image built, started with compose on a clean runner, and the unattended checks run against it through the published port (S1's open on the host's loopback, sign-in's and the library's after `setup` in the container); the static binaries started on a plain Debian container and an Alpine one, each answering `/api/info`; on arm64, the binary and image started on the arm64 runner. | the step's "done when", on both architectures |

### Not in F5

Signed binaries or images (decided separately); Windows images; a NAS's own package formats
(Synology, QNAP: the image covers them); automatic updates; OpenSubsonic.

### Steps (estimates)

1. Static Linux builds (x64, arm64) and macOS x64 in `service.yml`; the release attaching them
   (1 d).
2. The image (Dockerfile, the multi-arch build, GHCR on release); `JACKBIRD_LISTEN` and
   `JACKBIRD_MACHINE` with tests (1 d).
3. `deploy/compose.yaml` with restic and Caddy; the checks against the container (1 d).
4. `docs/install.md`, README, the walkthrough (1 d).

### Done when

F5's row: an instance installs from the docs on a clean machine and is reached at its address.

### Questions for the owner

1. **Open in a container**: the container's bridge counts as the machine, with the port published
   on the host's loopback only (recommended), or a container always needs sign-in before anything
   (`setup` first, from `docker compose exec`)?
2. **Linux binaries**: static, musl (recommended), or glibc, as today?
3. **Where the image is published**: GitHub's registry, `ghcr.io/jgermade/jackbird` (recommended:
   beside the releases, with the workflow's own token), or Docker Hub (an account and a token as a
   secret)?
4. **restic in compose**: the official image looping once a day (recommended), or left out of
   compose, documented only as in F4?
5. **macOS**: arm64 and x64 (recommended), or arm64 only?

`docs/install.md` says, and reached at its address by S1's unattended check (a folder opened, a
title saved, tracks played, the container restarted and the folder back). arm64, macOS x64 and the
push to `ghcr.io` (on the next release) are CI's. Findings below.

### What there is

- **The service**: `JACKBIRD_LISTEN` (where it listens; `0.0.0.0` in a container, with no
  `::1` or home-network listeners then), `JACKBIRD_MACHINE` (networks that count as this machine
  for an open instance; `gateway` is the container's default gateway, read from
  `/proc/net/route`), `JACKBIRD_LIBRARY` (the library's folder); in a container a loopback name
  with any port is its own `Host`. Tests: the host answered, another container's address and a
  rebinding name refused, networks parsed and matched.
- **`deploy/`**: `Dockerfile` (Alpine, CA certificates, the static binary of its
  architecture, user 1000, `/config` and `/library`, a health check), `compose.yaml` (the
  host's loopback, `JACKBIRD_USER`, music read only, restic in a `backup` profile once a day,
  Caddy commented), `.env.example`.
- **CI**: `ship.yml` (musl on native x64 and arm64 runners; each binary started on Debian and
  Alpine; the image started with compose; S1's check through it on x64; macOS x64 cross-built on
  the arm64 runner; the multi-arch image pushed to `ghcr.io/jgermade/jackbird` when
  `release.yml` calls it with `publish`); `release.yml` attaches the new binaries.
- **Docs**: `docs/install.md` (Docker Compose, a binary, the desktop app; the first owner by
  `setup`; outside home; restic; updating); README, architecture, development.

### Findings

1. **An open instance in a container refused everyone**: requests through a published port come
   from the container network's gateway; `JACKBIRD_MACHINE=gateway` makes exactly that address
   the machine, and another container on the same network is still refused (403, checked here).
2. **The published port is not the container's**: the `Host` check accepts a loopback name with
   any port in a container (the name is what keeps a rebinding page out).
3. **Files mounted from the host need their owner's id**: `JACKBIRD_USER` in compose
   (`id -u`:`id -g`), or the container's user 1000 cannot save into the music folder.
4. **`${VAR:?}` in compose fails even for a service in a profile not started**: restic checks its
   password itself.
5. Here, the image's `apk add` needed this session's proxy CA (a local copy of the Dockerfile
   only); CI's runners need nothing.

### Left to the owner, by hand

The image on a NAS or a Raspberry Pi from `ghcr.io` after the next release; a binary on a clean
Linux and on an Intel Mac; Caddy at a real address; restic's profile to a real repository.

## Extension — 2026-11-09 19:37 — E5, native output, and E6, mobile: implementation plans

**Summary**: The plans for E5 and E6 of `ROADMAP/2026-11-04/rust-tag-engine-and-tauri.md`, at the
owner's request (*"Sigue con E5 y E6"*). E5 is the desktop app playing through Rust instead of the
webview (decoding with `symphonia`, output with `cpal` plus each system's exclusive path), so a
24/96 FLAC reaches the DAC bit for bit; `<audio>` stays the default and the fallback. E6 is a spike
first, as the roadmap says; since S1 to F5, the phone's question is no longer only "how does it
read local files" but "client of the family instance, local files, or both". Eight decisions are
proposed for E5 and five for E6's spike; six questions are left to the owner. Nothing is
implemented until it is approved.

### E5: where it starts

- The player (`src/lib/player.ts`) drives one `<audio>` element. In the desktop app its source is
  the asset protocol (macOS, Windows) or the app's local media server (Linux, `src-tauri/src/media.rs`);
  in the browser, a `blob:` URL or the service's media URL.
- The spectrum routes the element through a Web Audio `AnalyserNode` (fftSize 1024) and
  `src/helpers/spectrum.ts` turns its 512 bins into bands.
- There is no volume control in the app: the system's is used.
- What the webview does to the sound: it decodes to float, mixes into the system mixer at the
  device's current rate (a 96 kHz file on a 48 kHz device is resampled), and the system mixer may
  apply its own volume and effects. None of that can be changed from a page (E-D8).

### E5: decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| E5-D1 | **A crate of its own, `crates/audio`**: decoding with `symphonia` (MPL-2.0; FLAC and MP3, the two formats the app opens) and output with `cpal` (Apache-2.0), on a thread of its own; it reads the file streamed from disk through `crates/core`'s root checks, never whole. The tag crates stay free of dependencies; this crate is beside them, as `store` is. | the roadmap's choice (2026-11-04); no GPL or LGPL code |
| E5-D2 | **Bit-perfect path per system**, each where the system offers it: **macOS**: the device's nominal rate set to the track's (CoreAudio), and *hog mode* (exclusive) as an option; **Windows**: WASAPI *exclusive* mode (cpal speaks only shared mode, so through the `wasapi` crate), with the device's format chosen to match the track's (24 in 32 if it takes only that, never dithered); **Linux**: ALSA's `hw:` device directly (no dmix, no PipeWire/PulseAudio resampling), the format and rate set to the track's. When the device cannot take the track's rate or depth, it says so and plays converted (shared), shown as such. | the roadmap's "exclusive / bit-perfect where the system offers it" |
| E5-D3 | **Two modes in the desktop app's settings**: *Browser* (today, the default) and *Native*; under Native, the output device and *Exclusive (bit-perfect)*. The Player shows what reaches the device: "FLAC 24 bit · 96 kHz → USB DAC · 96 kHz · bit-perfect", or "… → 48 kHz, converted" when it is not. | the owner sees whether it is bit-perfect, without a loopback |
| E5-D4 | **The player behind one interface**: `src/lib/player.ts` speaks to an *output* (load, play, pause, seek, position, duration, ended, error); `<audio>` is one, the native one is the other (Tauri commands, and events for position and the end of a track). The queue, the restore after a restart, the played counts (F3) and the refresh after a save are the same for both. Any native error falls back to `<audio>` for that track and says why. | `<audio>` stays the fallback, nothing else in the app changes |
| E5-D5 | **The analyser fed from Rust**: the output thread keeps the last 1024 samples it wrote (mono mix), and a command returns 512 magnitudes on the same 0–255 scale as `getByteFrequencyData`, with the same smoothing (0.8), from a small FFT written here (no dependency); `spectrum.ts` is unchanged. The spectrum is read from what was written, never on the path to the device. | the spectrum looks the same in both modes, and costs the sound nothing |
| E5-D6 | **Volume**: in exclusive mode, none in the app (the DAC's or amplifier's knob), as today; in shared native mode, the system's. No software volume in E5. | a software volume is the first thing that breaks bit-perfect |
| E5-D7 | **Gapless between tracks of the same format**, since the native output keeps the device open: the next track is decoded ahead and its first samples follow the last of the current one; a change of rate or depth reopens the device (a short silence, as any player). | it comes for free once the output is ours; albums that run on (live, classical) need it |
| E5-D8 | **Checks**: (1) every fixture and a new 24/96 and 16/44.1 FLAC decoded by `crates/audio` give exactly the PCM whose MD5 is in the file's own STREAMINFO (FLAC's own bit-perfect check), and MP3s give `ffmpeg`'s decode within the format's tolerance; (2) a *capture* output in the tests records what would reach the device: for a matching device format it is byte-identical to the decoded PCM (no conversion on our side), and a non-matching one is reported as converted; (3) on Linux in CI, ALSA's loopback (`snd-aloop`), where the runner's kernel has it: a 24/96 FLAC played through `hw:Loopback` in exclusive mode and recorded from the other end, compared bit for bit; (4) the desktop check (E4) plays a track in Native mode on the three systems (the runners have no sound card: macOS's and Windows' null devices where present, else the capture output, said in the log); (5) the owner, by hand, with a DAC that shows the rate. | the roadmap's "done when": bit-perfect on at least one system, by a loopback or the device's own display |

### E5: not in it

Native output in the service (the server's machine playing to its own speakers, as MPD); DSD and
DoP; ReplayGain and a software volume; an equaliser; upsampling; AAC/ALAC/Opus (the app opens MP3
and FLAC only); AirPlay/Chromecast/UPnP renderers.

### E5: steps (estimates)

1. `crates/audio`: decoding, the capture output, the FLAC MD5 check, the 24/96 fixture (2 d).
2. Output with `cpal` (shared) on the three systems; the player's output interface; the settings
   and the Player's line (2 d).
3. Exclusive paths: CoreAudio rate and hog mode, WASAPI exclusive, ALSA `hw:`; the loopback check
   on Linux (3 d).
4. The analyser from Rust; gapless; the desktop check in Native mode; the walkthrough (2 d).

### E5: done when

The roadmap's row: a 24/96 FLAC plays bit-perfect on at least one system, checked with a loopback
(CI, Linux) and the device's own sample-rate display (the owner, by hand).

### E6: where it starts

- When E6 was written (2026-11-04), a phone app meant the phone reading its own files: Android's
  Storage Access Framework (SAF: `content://` URIs, slow listing, no paths) and iOS's
  security-scoped bookmarks, both hard for `crates/core`, which works on paths.
- Since then the family instance exists (S1–F5): the library is on a machine at home, reached by
  HTTP with sign-in (codes, passkeys), media by short-lived tokens, the interface the same web app.
  A phone can be a *client* of it, as the web is, and most of the file-access problem goes away.
- Tauri v2 builds Android and iOS from the same `src-tauri/` (its `lib` is already a `cdylib`
  and `staticlib` for that). Nothing has been built for either yet.
- What a phone needs that a desktop does not: playback that keeps going with the screen off or
  the app in the background, lock-screen and headset controls, and songs kept on the phone for
  when there is no connection.

### E6: the spike's decisions (proposed)

| # | Proposal | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| E6-D1 | **The spike answers four questions, each with a build that runs**: (a) the web app in Tauri's Android and iOS webviews, Player view first, touch sizes (the `4vw` root already scales it); (b) **as a client of the family instance**: sign in to an instance by its address (code by email; passkeys in the webview where the system allows), browse the family library and *Mine*, play with media tokens; (c) **background playback and the lock screen**: Android needs a foreground service with a media session (a small Tauri plugin in Kotlin), iOS the `audio` background mode and an `AVAudioSession` of category *playback* (in Swift); whether the webview's `<audio>` keeps playing under them, or whether playback must move to native (E5's crate on Android with `cpal`'s AAudio, AVPlayer on iOS); (d) **local files**: opening a folder with SAF on Android and a security-scoped bookmark on iOS, listing and reading through a `VFSAdapter`, and how long a 2,000-song folder takes to list. | the roadmap's spike, widened to what the phone is now for |
| E6-D2 | **Built in CI**: Android (APK, unsigned, on the Ubuntu runner with the Android SDK and NDK) started in an emulator, with an unattended check like E4's (the page loads, connects to a service started on the runner, plays a track for a few seconds); iOS (a simulator build on the macOS runner, unsigned; started in the simulator with the same check if `xcrun simctl` allows it). On real phones, the owner by hand: an APK to install, and iOS through Xcode with the owner's own (free) account. | a spike that runs, not a paper |
| E6-D3 | **Offline songs** are measured, not built: how much a phone may keep (the app's own storage on both systems), and whether the service's existing media URLs with ranges are enough to download a song to it. | what the Player-first app will need, decided with data |
| E6-D4 | **OpenSubsonic weighed beside it**: the cost of the service also speaking the OpenSubsonic API (`ping`, `getArtists`, `getAlbum`, `stream`, `getCoverArt`, `star`, `scrobble`) so existing apps (Symfonium, Substreamer, play:Sub…) play the family library, against our own app. Measured as the size of a minimal implementation and checked with one free client in the emulator. | the record's own idea (2026-11-08); the cheapest way to the phone, with background play, lock screen and offline already solved by those apps |
| E6-D5 | **The spike ends with a record, before any build of the app**: what worked on each system, the numbers, the recommendation (our own app as a client, local files too or not, native playback or the webview's, OpenSubsonic or not), and E6b's plan for the owner to approve. Nothing of the spike ships; what is kept goes in behind a flag. | the roadmap's "decided by the spike, recorded before any build" |

### E6: steps of the spike (estimates)

1. `tauri android init` / `tauri ios init`, the builds in CI, the emulator and simulator checks
   (2 d).
2. Client of an instance: the address, sign-in, the library, playing (1 d).
3. Background playback and lock screen on Android and iOS (2 d).
4. SAF and bookmarks; OpenSubsonic's minimal API and a client against it; the record (2 d).

### Order

E5 first, then E6's spike, as the roadmap orders them; they share nothing but E6-D1(c)'s possible
use of `crates/audio` on Android. If the owner prefers the phone first, E6's spike can go first
(question 6).

### Questions for the owner

1. **E5, which system first** for the bit-perfect check by hand: which one has your DAC (macOS,
   Windows, Linux)? All three paths are built (recommended); the one with your DAC is checked
   first by hand.
2. **E5, the default**: *Browser* stays the default and Native is chosen in the settings
   (recommended), or Native is the desktop app's default, with `<audio>` only as the fallback?
3. **E5, exclusive by default** when Native is chosen (recommended: yes, it is why one chooses
   Native; other apps' sound is silenced while it plays), or shared by default with exclusive as
   an option?
4. **E5, gapless** in E5 (recommended), or later?
5. **E6, what the phone is for**: a client of the family instance first, local files only weighed
   (recommended), or both on equal terms? And **OpenSubsonic**: weighed in the spike (recommended),
   left out, or built instead of our own app?
6. **The order**: E5 then E6 (recommended, as the roadmap), or E6's spike first?
